[9:45] and then get out of the way. When it's too tight, it holds the flapper open just a crack. Like a door that never quite latches. And water seeps through all day long. [10:15] The fix is quick. With the flapper sitting closed, move the clip on the handle arm until there's about half an inch of slack in the chain. Enough that it droops a little without piling up. The tally doesn't budge. Number five, the float. If your toilet flushes weak, swirls lazy, and sometimes needs a second flush to clear, check the water level in the tank before you blame the toilet.

[11:17] The water stored in that tank is the push behind every flush. And more tanks than you'd think are set too low. Sometimes a past owner turned it down to save water. [11:48] a lazy flush. Look on the inside wall of the tank for a fill line marked in the porcelain. And if there is one, go by it. [0:00] You might think your kitchen sink can handle just about anything, but there are a few common things people pour down their drains every single day that are quietly destroying their pipes from the inside out. After three decades of fixing clogs and replacing damaged plumbing, I can tell you that most of these expensive repairs were completely avoidable. Today we are going over five things you should never pour down your drain. And

[0:31] number five might surprise you because most people have no idea it is even a problem. Plus, stick around for a bonus tip at the end that could save you hundreds of dollars a year. The first thing you should never pour down your drain is cooking grease, fats, and oils. Now, I know this one might sound obvious, but you would be amazed how many homeowners still do this every single day without thinking twice. You

[1:03] finish cooking up some bacon on a Saturday morning and that hot grease is just sitting there in the pan looking like a liquid. So, it seems perfectly fine to just rinse it right down the sink with some hot water, right? Well, here is what is actually happening inside your pipes. That grease might be a liquid when it is hot, but the moment it hits the cooler walls of your drain line, it starts to

[1:35] solidify. It clings to the inside of the pipe like a coating. And every time you pour more grease down there, you are adding another layer. Over weeks and months, that buildup gets thicker and thicker, narrowing the opening inside your pipe until water can barely squeeze through. Now add in little bits of food particles, soap residue, and other debris that get caught in that sticky layer, and you have got yourself a

[2:07] fullblown clog forming. Professional plumbers call these fatbergs. And in some city sewer systems, they have found grease blockages the size of small cars. In your home, the result is usually slow draining sinks, foul odors coming up from the drain, and eventually a backup that can flood your kitchen. getting a professional to hydrojet. A grease clogged line can cost anywhere from 300

[2:37] to over,000. And if the buildup has been there long enough to cause corrosion or pipe damage, you could be looking at several thousand in replacement costs. So, what should you do instead? Let that grease cool down in the pan. Once it solidifies, scrape it into a container. An old coffee can works great. And toss it in the trash. For smaller amounts of oily residue, wipe the pan out with a

[3:09] paper towel before washing it. It takes about 30 seconds, and it will save you a world of trouble down the road. The second thing you should never pour down your drain is chemical drain cleaners. And this one is a real eyeopener because most people think they are actually helping their pipes when they reach for that bottle of drain cleaner at the hardware store. Products like these are marketed as a

[3:41] quick fix. And sure, they might seem to work in the short term. You pour them down a slow drain, wait a few minutes, and the water starts flowing again. Problem solved, right? Not even close. Here is what those chemical cleaners are actually doing inside your pipes. Most of these products work by creating an intense chemical reaction that generates heat, sometimes reaching temperatures

[4:12] close to 200° F. That heat is what dissolves the clog, but it is also eating away at your pipes. If you have got older metal pipes, the harsh chemicals accelerate corrosion from the inside. And if you have PVC plastic pipes, that extreme heat can actually soften and warp the pipe walls, weakening the joints and creating spots where leaks

[4:42] will eventually develop. The real kicker is that these products rarely clear the entire clog. They might burn a small channel through the blockage, which is enough to get water flowing again temporarily, but the bulk of the clog is still sitting there. So, what happens? The drain slows down again in a few weeks. You pour in more chemicals and the cycle repeats. Each time your pipes take more damage. I have

[5:14] seen homes where years of chemical drain cleaner used left the pipes so corroded and brittle that entire sections had to be replaced. We are talking about $2,000 to $5,000 or more in repair costs. All because of a product that costs about $8 a bottle. Instead of chemical cleaners, invest in a simple drain snake or a hand crank augur. You can pick one up at any

[5:45] hardware store for around 20 to $30 and it will physically remove the clog without damaging your pipes at all. For kitchen sinks, a good enzymebased drain maintainer used once a month can help break down organic buildup safely. And if you have got a stubborn clog that you just cannot clear yourself, call a professional. A plumber with a proper drain machine

[6:15] can clear almost any blockage for a fraction of what it would cost to replace damaged pipes. The third thing you should never pour down your drain is coffee grounds. Now, I cannot tell you how many times I have been called out to a home with a badly clogged kitchen drain. And when I ask the homeowner what they have been putting down the sink, they mention

[6:45] coffee grounds like it is the most normal thing in the world. And honestly, I understand why coffee grounds look so fine and harmless. You rinse out your French press or dump your coffee filter over the sink. The grounds seem to wash right down with the water and everything appears to be fine. But here is what is really going on beneath that drain opening. Coffee grounds do not dissolve in water, not even a little bit. Unlike

[7:18] soap or food scraps that might eventually break down, coffee grounds stay intact. When they wash into your drain line, they settle at the bottom of the pipe, especially in any spot where the pipe curves, dips, or connects to another line. Over time, they pack together almost like wet sand. And just like wet sand, once they settle and compact, water has a very hard time pushing through.

[7:50] What makes this even worse is that coffee grounds love to stick to grease. If you have got even a thin film of grease residue on the inside of your pipes, and almost every kitchen drain does, those grounds will cling to it and start building up layer by layer. Before you know it, you have got a dense, sludgy blockage that is incredibly stubborn to clear. A lot of people also assume that running the garbage disposal

[8:21] will take care of the grounds. It will not. The disposal might chop up larger food scraps, but coffee grounds are already tiny. They pass right through the disposal and into your drain line, where the real problem begins. Having a plumber come out to clear a coffee ground. clog typically runs between $150 and $500 depending on how deep the blockage is and how long it has been building up.

[8:52] So instead of rinsing those grounds down the sink, toss them in the trash. Better yet, add them to a compost pile if you have got a garden. Coffee grounds are fantastic for composting and your plants will thank you. just keep them out of your pipes. Now, if you're finding this video helpful so far, I'd really appreciate it if you'd hit that like button and subscribe to the channel. It helps this

[9:24] video reach more homeowners like yourself who would also find this information helpful. All right, let's continue. The fourth thing you should never pour down your drain is flour and starchy foods like pasta, rice, and potatoes. This one catches a lot of people offguard because we tend to think of these as simple harmless foods. But when it comes to your plumbing, starchy substances are a real nightmare waiting

[9:55] to happen. Let me start with flour because it is probably the worst offender that nobody talks about. Think about what happens when you mix flour with water. You get a thick sticky paste. That is essentially what is happening inside your drain pipe. When you rinse flour down the sink, whether you are cleaning off a cutting, bored after rolling out dough, rinsing a mixing

[10:25] bowl, or washing your hands after breading chicken cutlets, that flour hits the water in your drain and immediately starts turning into a gluey mess. It coats the inside of the pipe and hardens as it dries, creating a layer that grabs onto everything else that comes down the drain. Now, let us talk about pasta and rice. Most people know that pasta and rice expand when

[10:56] they absorb water. What a lot of folks do not realize is that they keep expanding even after you are done cooking them. When you dump leftover pasta or rice down the drain or into the garbage disposal, those starchy pieces continue to soak up moisture inside your pipes. They swell up, press against the pipe walls, and create a blockage that water cannot push through easily. Pasta

[11:26] is especially bad because it gets soft and sticky as it expands, clumping together into a mass that behaves almost like a plug. And potatoes are no better. Potato peels are notorious for causing garbage disposal jams because they are thin and slippery. They slip right past the blades and form a starchy film inside the drain line. Mashed potatoes are even worse since they are basically a thick paste going

[11:58] straight into your pipes. The repair bill for a starch related clog usually runs between $150 and $600. And if the buildup has had time to harden and compact, it can require professional hydrojetting to fully clear. The fix here is simple. Scrape all pasta, rice, potato peels, and flour residue into the trash or compost bin before washing your

[12:28] dishes. If you have got a strainer basket for your sink drain, use it. They cost just a few dollars and they catch all of this stuff before it ever reaches your pipes. Trust me, a $2 strainer is a lot cheaper than a $600 plumber visit. [clears throat] Continuing with the final sections now, the fifth and final thing you should never pour down your drain is paint and solvents.

[12:58] And this is the one that surprises most homeowners because a lot of people have been washing paint brushes and rollers in the sink for years without giving it a second thought. Whether you just finished a weekend project painting the spare bedroom or you are touching up some trim in the hallway, it feels natural to just rinse everything off under the faucet. But what you are sending down that drain is

[13:29] far more damaging than you might realize. Let us start with latex paint since that is the most common type used in homes today. A lot of people assume that because latex paint is water-based, it is safe to wash down the sink. And in very small amounts with plenty of running water, some municipalities do technically allow it. But here is the problem. Even water-based latex paint

[14:00] contains binders, pigments, and chemical additives that do not break down easily. When you rinse a roller full of paint in the sink, that paint flows into your drain line and starts to coat the inside of the pipe. As it dries, it hardens into a film that builds up over time. After a few painting projects, you can end up with a significant layer of dried paint, narrowing your pipes from the

[14:31] inside. Now, oil-based paints and solvents like paint thinner, tarpentine, and mineral spirits are a whole different level of problem. These substances are genuinely hazardous. They can eat away at rubber gaskets and seals inside your plumbing connections, causing leaks. They can damage PVC pipe over time. And if you are on a septic system, pouring solvents

[15:02] down the drain can kill the beneficial bacteria that your septic tank relies on to break down waste, which can lead to a full septic system failure, costing you $10,000 or more to repair. On top of all the plumbing damage, there is a legal side to this as well. In most states, it is actually illegal to pour paint and solvents down the drain. These chemicals can contaminate the

[15:32] water supply and harm the environment. And municipalities take this seriously. Fines can range from a few hundred to several thousand depending on where you live. So, what is the right way to handle paint cleanup? For latex paint, wipe your brushes and rollers on in newspaper or old rags first to get as much paint off as possible. Then wash them in a bucket of water instead of the sink. Let that bucket sit until the

[16:04] paint settles to the bottom. Pour off the clear water on top into your yard away from storm drains and throw the dried paint solids in the trash. For oilbased paints and solvents, never pour them down any drain. Period. Store used solvents in a sealed container and take them to your local household hazardous waste collection facility. Most communities hold collection events

[16:36] several times a year, and many hardware stores will accept leftover paint as well. A little bit of extra effort in cleanup can protect your pipes, your septic system, your wallet, and the environment. And now for the bonus tip that professional plumbers swear by. Chemical drain cleaners, those bottles you see at the hardware store with the skull and crossbones, they are not the answer. I cannot stress this

[2:36] enough. Those products use caustic chemicals that can eat away at your pipe walls, especially if you've got an older home with cast iron or galvanized steel pipes. [3:08] we're going to do instead is prevent the buildup from ever getting started. And that brings us to the core habit. This is the big one. The monthly hot water and washing soda flush. Arm and Hammer makes a box of it called super washing soda. It usually costs around three to four dollars for a 55 oz box, and that

[3:39] box will last you about two years of monthly treatments. So, we're talking pennies per month. Now, washing soda is not the same thing as baking soda. Baking soda is sodium bicarbonate, and it's much milder. Washing soda has a higher pH, which means it's more alkaline and it does a much better job of dissolving grease, soap scum, and organic buildup inside your pipes. It won't damage your pipes, it won't create

[4:10] toxic fumes, and it's been around for over a hundred years. Here's exactly how to do it. Once a month, pick a night when you're done using the sink for the evening. Boil a full kettle or a large pot of water. While the water is heating up, pour about half a cup of washing soda directly into the drain. Then slowly pour the boiling water down after it. The hot water activates the washing soda and pushes it down through your pipes, dissolving and loosening any

[4:40] buildup clinging to the pipe walls. The reason you do this at night is simple. You want that solution sitting in your pipes for as long as possible, working overnight while nobody's running water. By morning, the next time you turn on the faucet, everything that was loosened just washes away. Do this on every drain in your house. Kitchen sink, bathroom sinks, shower drains, even the bathtub. I do mine on the first of every month. I set a calendar reminder on my phone and

[5:10] the whole process takes maybe 15 minutes for the entire house. Now, if you'd rather use an enzyme-based drain treatment instead, that works, too. Enzyme cleaners use natural bacteria that literally eat organic material inside your pipes. They're completely safe for all pipe types. You pour them in at night, same idea, let them work while you sleep. They cost a little more than washing soda, usually around eight to twelve dollars a bottle, but a bottle lasts several months. Either method

[5:41] works. The key is consistency. Pick one and do it every single month. Next up, and this one is so simple it almost feels too easy. Put mesh strainers on every single drain in your house. Every one. Kitchen sink, bathroom sinks, shower, bathtub. This is your first line of defense, and it's the cheapest plumbing investment you'll ever make. [6:14] anything that has no business going down your pipes. For bathroom sinks, they make small stainless steel mesh discs that sit right over the drain. They catch hair, toothpaste chunks, and soap pieces. For the shower and bathtub, you want a flat silicone or stainless steel hair catcher. [6:44] or online. A full set for every drain in a typical house will run you about 10 to 15 dollars total. Clean them out every few days or whenever you notice build-up. In the kitchen, I empty mine after every big cooking session. In the shower, I pull the hair off after every shower. Takes about 3 seconds. That little mesh screen is stopping debris before it ever enters your pipes, which means your monthly washing soda flush has less work to do. The two habits work

Not even with hot water running. I don't care what anybody told you. Grease does not wash away. Here's what actually happens. You pour hot grease down the sink with the hot water running. And sure, it flows right through the trap and out of sight. Feels like it worked, right? But about 15 to

[8:14] 20 ft downstream, where the pipe runs horizontal through your floor or wall, that grease hits cooler water and cooler pipe walls. It starts to solidify. It coats the inside of the pipe like candle wax building up on a candle holder. And every time you send more grease down there, another thin layer gets added. Over months, that build up narrows the pipe until nothing gets through. And the worst part is this happens in a section

[8:46] of pipe you can't reach with a plunger or a basic drain snake. You need a professional with a power auger to clear it. And that's a service call. So, here's what to do instead. [9:17] towel before they go in the sink or dishwasher. It takes 5 seconds and it removes 90% of the grease before water ever touches the dish. And here's one more habit that goes hand in hand with this. After every use of your kitchen sink, especially after washing dishes, run the hot water for about 30 seconds. Just let it flow. That hot water chases everything through the trap and further down the line while it's still warm

[9:47] enough to keep moving. It's a tiny thing, but over time it makes a real difference. Let's talk about two more spots in your house that need attention. Your garbage disposal and your toilets. If you have a garbage disposal, it needs a monthly cleaning. Food particles get trapped under the splash guard and around the grinding plate. And over time they rot and create both odors and build-up that feeds into your drain pipe. Here's my method. Once a month, dump about two cups of ice cubes into

[10:19] the disposal followed by a half cup of coarse salt, like kosher salt. Run the disposal with cold water for about 30 seconds. The ice and salt scrub the inside walls and the grinding plate clean. After that, cut a lemon or lime in half and grind both halves through the disposal with cold water running. That takes care of the odor. And remember, always run cold water when using the disposal, not hot. Cold water keeps any grease in the food solid so

[10:50] the disposal can chop it up and send it through. Hot water melts the grease and then you're back to the same problem we just talked about. While we're at it, here's what should never go down a disposal. Pasta, rice, potato peels, eggshells, coffee grounds, celery, or any fibrous vegetable. These either expand with water, create a starchy paste, or wrap around the grinding mechanism. Now, your toilets. This is simple. The toilet is designed for two things: human waste and toilet paper.

[11:22] That's it. Nothing else. Not facial tissues, not cotton balls, not dental floss, not cat litter, and especially not so-called flushable wipes. Those wipes are one of the biggest lies in household products. They do not break down like toilet paper. [11:52] in your sewer line that can cost $500 or more to clear. Throw them in the trash every single time. Now, let me explain why your plumber has never sat you down and told you all of this. [13:27] It's just economics. The information is out there if you go looking for it, but nobody in the industry has any incentive to push it until now. So, consider this your insider tip. Let's talk about some common problems you might run into and how to handle them. First, if you start this monthly routine and your drain is still running slow, that probably means you've already got significant buildup in the pipe. The washing soda flush is a maintenance habit, not a cure for an

[13:58] existing clog. If you've got a slow drain right now, you may need to start with a good manual snaking to clear the existing buildup and then begin the monthly routine to keep it from coming back. Second, if you hear gurgling sounds when water drains, that usually means there's a venting issue, not a clog. Every drain in your house is connected to a vent pipe that goes up through your roof. If that vent gets blocked by leaves, a bird's nest, or ice

[14:29] in the winter, air can't flow properly and you get that gurgling sound. This isn't something the washing soda will fix. You'd want to check your roof vents or call a professional to inspect them. Third, if you're getting a bad smell from a drain you don't use very often, like a basement floor drain or a guest bathroom sink, the problem is almost certainly a dried out P-trap. The P-trap is that curved section of pipe under every drain. It holds a small amount of

[15:00] water that acts as a seal against sewer gases. If a drain sits unused for a few weeks, that water evaporates and sewer gas comes right up into your your house. The fix is dead simple. Just run water in that drain for about 15 seconds every couple of weeks to keep the trap full. Fourth, if multiple drains in your house are slow at the same time, that's a red flag. It usually means the blockage is in your main sewer line, not in individual branch drain. This is one

[15:31] situation where you absolutely want to call a professional. A main line blockage can involve tree roots, a collapsed pipe, or a major grease build-up deep in the system. Don't try to handle this one yourself. And fifth, if you do everything right and still get an occasional slow drain, don't panic. [16:01] washing soda treatment that week, and if it doesn't improve within a day or two, a simple hand snake from the hardware store, about $15, will usually take care of it. [16:31] behind the faucet. On most sinks, you can just grab it and pull it straight up, and it lifts right out. On some models, you might need to twist it a quarter turn first, then pull up. Once it's out, look at the bottom of it. I can almost guarantee you're going to find a disgusting clump of hair, soap scum, and slime wrapped around the base of that stopper. This is the number one cause of slow bathroom sink drains, and it has nothing to do with what's deep in

[17:02] your pipe. It's sitting right there, 2 in below the drain opening, choking the flow. Clean it off with a paper towel. You can use an old toothbrush to scrub the rod and the stopper or Then look down into the drain opening. You'll probably see more gunk clinging to the pivot rod that connects to the stopper mechanism. Clean that out, too. Reassemble, push the stopper back in, and run the water. I promise you that

[17:32] drain is going to flow like it's brand new. Do this once a month, same day you do your washing soda flush, and you will likely never have a slow bathroom sink drain again. [13:55] your tank. Your flapper is made of rubber. Your gaskets and seals are made of rubber. The diaphragm inside your fill valve is made of rubber. Concentrated chlorine degrades rubber. [14:25] It's one of the most common causes of phantom running toilet, where the toilet randomly runs for a few seconds every hour because the flapper has been eaten away and no longer seals. [14:56] chlorine-based tank additives right in their documentation. Hydrogen peroxide at 3% concentration does not do this. It sanitizes, it kills mold and

[15:26] bacteria, it loosens scale, and it breaks down into water and oxygen. It doesn't sit in there for weeks attacking your rubber parts. You put it in, it works overnight, you flush it away in the morning. That's the critical difference. It does the job without the damage. Check that fill line again. If the water level in your tank is more than an inch below the fill line, your tank isn't holding enough water to generate a strong flush. You can adjust this by turning the screw on top of your

[18:32] fill valve a quarter turn at a time, then flush and check. The water should refill to right at that fill line. Fifth, if you smell a sulfur or rotten egg odor coming from the tank, that's hydrogen sulfide, and it's a sign of anaerobic bacteria in your water supply. The peroxide treatment will knock it back temporarily, but if it keeps returning, you may want to have your water tested,

[19:03] especially if you're on well water. A whole-house treatment may be needed to address the source. Here's your maintenance schedule, and I want to keep this simple because if it's complicated, you won't do it. Once a month, pour 1 to 2 cups of 3% hydrogen peroxide into the tank before bed. Don't flush until morning. That's it. 2 minutes of effort, about 50 cents in

[19:33] peroxide, once a month. I do mine on the 1st of every month so I don't forget. Some folks set a reminder on their phone. If you're on well water or you notice build-up returning faster, you can bump it to every 2 or 3 weeks. But for most people on municipal water, monthly is perfect. Think of it the same way you think about changing your furnace filter. It's a small task that prevents

[20:03] big problems. Now, for that bonus tip I promised you at the beginning. Since you already have the tank lid off, take 30 seconds to inspect your flapper. This is something most people never do until the toilet starts running on its own. And by then, you've already wasted a significant amount of water. Press down on the flapper gently with your finger. It should feel soft and flexible, like a

[20:34] rubber ball. If it feels stiff, rigid, or crunchy, it's deteriorating and needs to be replaced soon. Now, lift it up and look at the seat it rests on, that ring at the bottom of the tank. Run your finger around it. It should be smooth and clean. If there's mineral build-up on that seat, the flapper can't seal properly even if the flapper itself is still in good shape. A little scrub with your tank brush and

[21:05] some peroxide will clean that seat right up. Here's the real pro move. While you've got the flapper lifted, look at the underside of it. If you see warping, pitting, or if black residue comes off on your fingers, replace it now. Don't wait. A new flapper is one of the cheapest parts in plumbing, usually $5 to $8, and it's one of the easiest to install.

[21:35] You just unhook the old one from the ears on the overflow tube, hook the new one on, and attach the chain to the flush handle lever with about half an inch of slack. The whole job takes less than 2 minutes. Doing this flapper check every time you do your monthly peroxide treatment means you'll catch a failing flapper before it starts wasting water. Over a year, a leaking flapper can waste

[22:05] thousands of gallons. So, that 30-second inspection is genuinely worth your time. Before you go, I want to make sure you know about my new book, 100 old house fixes that save you thousands. [0:30] because of habits most people don't even realize are causing problems. In 45 years of plumbing, I have never had a clogged drain in my own home. Not once. You just need the right habits and a little bit of knowledge about what's actually happening inside your pipes.

[1:31] And that's exactly what I'm going to give you today. So, let's start with understanding why drains clog in the first place. Here's something most people never think about. Your drain pipes aren't perfectly smooth inside. Even brand new PVC has tiny ridges where the joints connect. And if you've got an older home with cast iron pipes, the inside surface is even rougher. Now, picture this. Every time you wash

[2:02] something down the drain, a tiny film of residue sticks to those surfaces. Grease, soap, hair, food particles, toothpaste, shaving cream, all of it leaves behind a thin layer. And that layer is sticky. So, the next bit of debris that comes through sticks to it. Then the next layer sticks to that. Over time, you're building up a narrower and narrower opening inside that pipe.

[2:32] It's like plaque building up in an artery. The water still flows, just a little slower each week until one day something catches and blocks the whole thing. That's your out of nowhere clog. It was actually months in the making. So, the key to never having a clogged drain isn't about unclogging. It's about never letting that build up start in the first place.

[3:02] And that begins in your kitchen. The kitchen sink is the number one place where clogs start. And it almost always comes down to one thing. Grease. Now, I'm not just talking about pouring a pan full of bacon fat down the drain. Most people know not to do that. I'm talking about the grease you don't even think about. The oily residue on your dinner plates, the butter that melts off your knife,

[3:34] the little bit of salad dressing left in the bowl. All of that goes down the drain and coats the inside of your pipes. Here's what I do and what I've done for 45 years. Before I wash any dish, pot, or pan that had anything greasy or oily on it, I wipe it down with a paper towel first. Just a quick wipe. Takes about 30 seconds. That paper towel goes in the trash. And now 90% of the grease never even

[4:07] makes it to the drain. After that, I always run hot water for about 30 seconds after I'm done washing dishes. Not warm, hot. As hot as your tap will go. That helps flush any remaining residue down and through the pipe before it has a chance to cool and stick to the walls. >> [sighs] >> The other thing I never do is put starchy foods down the garbage disposal. Rice, pasta, potato peels,

[4:38] those things expand with water and turn into a paste inside your pipes. I don't care what the disposal can grind up. It's not about the grinding. It's about what happens after it gets past the disposal and into the pipe. Scrape those into the trash every single time. Now, let's move to the bathroom because this is where the second most common clogs happen. And the culprit is almost

[5:08] always hair combined with soap scum. Here's what most people don't realize. Hair by itself doesn't usually cause a clog. And soap scum by itself doesn't usually cause a clog. But when you combine them, the soap acts like glue that binds the hair together into a thick web inside the pipe. That web catches everything else that comes through. And before you know it, you've got a

[5:38] full blockage sitting about 8 to 12 in below the drain opening. What I do in every shower and every bathroom sink is simple. I clean the visible drain area once a week. In the shower, that means pulling up the drain cover and removing any hair that's collected around the crossbars. It takes about 15 seconds. It's not glamorous, but it works.

[6:09] For the bathroom sink, I pull out the pop-up stopper, which just lifts right out on most sinks, and I clean the hair and gunk off the bottom [snorts] of it. You'd be amazed how much collects on that little stopper rod. I also switched to liquid body wash and liquid hand soap years ago instead of bar soap. Bar soap creates much more scum residue than liquid soap does. That one change alone made a noticeable

[6:41] difference in how clean my bathroom drain stayed over time. By the way, if you're finding this advice helpful, you should know it's just a small sample of what I've put into my new guide, The Ultimate Plumbing Playbook. Once a month, I do a hot water flush on every drain in the house. Here's exactly how. I plug the drain, fill the sink or tub all the way up with the hottest water the tap will produce, and then I pull the plug all at once.

[7:44] That full volume of hot water rushing through the pipe at once creates a powerful flush that pushes out any early buildup before it has a chance to harden. For the kitchen sink, I do both sides if it's a double basin. For the bathtub, I fill it about a quarter of the way, which is still a huge volume of water. The whole process takes about 5 minutes per drain, and I usually do it on the

[8:14] first Saturday of the month while I'm having my coffee. It's become second nature at this point. Now, one important note. If you have older PVC pipes, make sure you're using hot tap water, not boiling water from a kettle. Boiling water can soften PVC joints over time. Hot tap water, which is usually around 120° F, is perfectly safe and plenty effective.

[8:44] Now, let's talk about drain strainers because almost everybody has them. And almost nobody uses them correctly. A drain strainer only works if it actually catches debris before it enters the pipe. Sounds obvious, right? But here's what I see all the time. People buy a strainer, set it in the drain, and then never empty it. So, it fills up, water pools on top, and

[9:14] they either push the debris through or just take the strainer out because it's it's annoying. Here's my approach. I use stainless steel mesh strainers, not the ones with big holes punched in them. The mesh catches everything, including small food particles that the bigger strainers let right through. I buy them at any hardware store for about $3 to $4 each. And I empty them after every use.

[9:45] In the kitchen, that means after every time I wash dishes. In the shower, after every shower. It takes literally 5 seconds to dump it in the trash. The trick is making it a habit so you don't even think about it. I'd rather spend 5 seconds emptying a strainer than 5 hours fighting a clog. This next one is my secret weapon and it's the thing that really sets my drain

[10:15] maintenance apart from what most people do. Every month, right after I do my hot water flush, I pour an enzyme-based drain treatment into every drain in the house. Now, let me be clear. I'm not talking about chemical drain cleaners like the ones you see in the big plastic bottles at the store. Those products use harsh chemicals like sodium hydroxide or sulfuric acid and

[10:45] over time, they can damage your pipes, especially if you have older cast iron or even PVC. I never use them and I never recommend them. Enzyme treatments are completely different. They use natural bacteria and enzymes that literally eat the organic material building up inside your pipes. Grease, hair, soap scum, food residue, all of it. The bacteria break it down naturally

[11:16] over the course of several hours. The brand I've used for years is called Bio Clean, but there are several good enzyme treatments out there. You can find them at most plumbing supply stores or online. Here's how I use it. I mix about 2 Tbsp of the powder into a cup of warm water, not hot, just warm, because you don't want to kill the bacteria. I pour it down the drain right before bed so it

[11:46] sits in the pipe overnight without any water running through. By morning, those enzymes have been working for 8 hours breaking down anything that was starting to build up. I do this on the same day as my hot water flush. Flush first to clear the easy stuff, then enzyme treatment overnight to handle the rest. It's a one-two punch that keeps pipes absolutely spotless inside.

[12:16] Now, let's talk about what to do if you notice a drain starting to slow down, because even with the best habits, sometimes things happen. [12:49] The first thing I do is try the hot water flush I already described. Fill it up, release it all at once. [13:21] I use one with a 1/4-in cable that's about 15 ft long, which is enough to reach past the trap and into the branch line where most clogs form. After snaking, I always do a hot water flush followed

[13:52] by an enzyme treatment to clean up whatever the snake loosened but didn't pull out. Let's talk about some common drain problems you might run into and how to solve them. First, if you notice a gurgling sound when water drains, that's usually a venting issue, not a clog. Your drain pipes need air to flow properly and that air comes from vent pipes that go up through your roof.

[14:22] If a vent gets blocked by leaves or bird's nest, you'll hear gurgling. Check your roof vents if you can do it safely or have someone take a look for you. Second, if you get a bad smell coming from a drain you don't use very often, like a guest bathroom or basement floor drain, the problem is almost always a dried-out P-trap. That curved pipe under the drain is

[14:52] supposed to hold water that blocks sewer gas from coming up. If it dries out, you smell the gas. The fix is dead simple. Just pour about 2 cups of water down that drain every couple of weeks to keep the trap full. Third, if your kitchen sink drains slowly but only on one side of a double basin, the problem is usually a partial clog right at the T-fitting where the two basins connect.

[15:22] A short snake or even a good hot water flush will usually clear it. Fourth, if every drain in the house is slow at the same time, that's a main line issue and that's one situation where I'd recommend calling a professional. They'll run a camera down the line to see exactly what's going on, whether it's root intrusion, a belly in the pipe, or a major buildup. Trying to fix a main line issue yourself

[15:54] can make things worse. Fifth, if you've been using chemical drain cleaners regularly and your drains are still slow, stop using them immediately. There's a good chance they've actually made things worse by creating a hardened chemical residue inside the pipe that catches debris even more easily. [16:26] treatments to dissolve that chemical buildup. [16:56] The P-trap is that curved section of pipe directly under every sink. Most modern P-traps have slip joint connections that you can unscrew completely by hand. No tools needed. I put a small bucket underneath, unscrew both connections, and pull the trap straight down. Then I take it outside, rinse it out with a garden hose, and wipe the inside with an old bottle brush.

[17:26] You would not believe what comes out of these things, even in a well-maintained home. A thin layer of dark sludge that's been slowly accumulating. I usually do it in the spring and fall, the same weekends I change my furnace

[17:58] filters and check my water heater anode rod. It's just a part of the routine. [9:48] And here is the mistake that quietly guts that rating. The drill bit. Every anchor system names an exact bit size in its instructions, and that size is part of the engineering. [10:19] And while we are on it, do not lean on the bolt like a gorilla when you tighten. Snug, then a quarter turn. Overtightening crushes the very gypsum you are depending on. That part matters. Drilling the tile is where most homeowners freeze up, so let me take the fear out of it. Tile is hard but brittle, like a candy shell over a soft center, and it only cracks when you treat it like concrete. Three rules. First, put a strip of masking tape over each mark because a

[10:51] drill bit on slick glaze skates like a dress shoe on ice, and tape gives the tip something to bite. Second, use a carbide or diamond tip bit, the hard-tipped kind made for tile and stone. And if your drill has a hammer setting, turn it off. Because hammering is for concrete, and hammering tile is how you spiderweb a wall you cannot easily replace. Third, go slow with light pressure, and keep the bit cool with a spray of water or a wet sponge held at the hole.

[11:22] Start the drill at low speed until the tip seats, then let it grind its way through at its own pace. A good tile hole is a 2-minute hole, not a 20-second hole. And that patience is the full price of a wall that still looks perfect when you finish. The moment you feel the bit break through the tile into softer board behind it, ease off. You are through. Before any anchor goes in, every hole gets silicone. And I mean every hole.

[11:52] Because this step is the difference between a safe bar and a slow disaster. Tile exists to keep water out of the wall. And the moment you drill it, you have opened a doorway that the shower will throw water at every single day. Water inside a wall grows mold, rots the framing, and softens the exact gypsum your anchors are clamped to. And I have seen more bathroom walls ruined by one unsealed hole than by any burst pipe.

[12:22] So, run a bead of pure silicone, the kitchen and bath kind, into each hole and around each anchor as it goes in. Then a thin bead around each flange where it meets the tile. Then give it a day. Silicone wants about 24 hours of cure before a shower rains on it. So, mount today, test today, and let tomorrow morning be the first wet run. Mounting is the easy part now. Anchors through the holes, wings open

[12:54] flat behind the wall, flanges on, bolts snug plus a quarter turn. Cover plates clipped over the hardware so the whole thing looks factory clean. Grab the bar with both hands and pull, straight out from the wall, then straight down with real weight behind it, more than polite, the way a falling person would.

[13:24] I am doing exactly that right now, full body weight, And this bar is not having a conversation about it. Not a creak. Not a whisper of flex. That is what code strength feels like. 250 lb required, and these anchors tested past 300. If your bar moves at all, even a hair, stop and find the cause. [1:33] greedier. It runs that exhaust through a second heat exchanger, which is simply another set of metal passages that pull even more warmth out of the exhaust before it leaves the house. [2:38] same idea as the U bend under your kitchen sink. A curve that always holds a little water so gases stay on their side of the fence. Now picture the conditions inside that line. [3:10] Here is where the shutdown comes in. And this is the most useful idea in the whole video. So, let it land. Your furnace is watching that water. VIDEO: Borax vs Baking Soda vs Washing Soda — A 45-Year Plumber's Verdict
Channel: Mark Silverbridge Plumbing (own) | Format: FACELESS
Selection: 5 most-viewed long-form uploads, last 24 months

[0:00] Last night, you poured half a box of baking soda down the kitchen sink, chased it with vinegar, and watched the foam climb up out of the drain like something serious was finally happening. This morning, the sink drains exactly as slow as it did the day before. Go back and look at that shelf. 2 ft from the baking soda in a box that looks almost identical, sat a product that would have actually worked. same aisle, same price range, and measured honestly about a

[0:30] thousand times stronger. In 35 years of pulling apart drain lines under kitchen sinks, I have watched more homeowners waste money on the weakest of those three boxes than on any other product in the house. So, let me line all three up and hand each one a job. Stay to the end because there is a fourth option that turns the weakest box on that shelf into the strongest one in your own oven for nothing. Here is why the confusion exists in the first place. Walk down the

[1:00] laundry aisle of any grocery store and you will find them within about 6 ft of each other. An orange box of baking soda, a green box of borax, a yellow box of washing soda. Every one of them is white powder. Everyone is a sodium compound out of the same chemical family and they all cost somewhere between $1 and $9. That is where the similarity stops. What actually separates them is alkalinity. Alkalinity is just the opposite of

[1:31] acidity and the way we measure it is a scale called pH. Vinegar is acidic. Lie is alkaline. Plain water sits in the middle at seven. The higher the number climbs above seven, the more aggressively that substance can attack fats and oils. Baking soda sits at about 8. Borax sits at about 9.2. Washing soda sits at about 11. Now, if you are looking at 8 and 11 and thinking those are pretty close together, that is

[2:02] the whole misunderstanding right there. That scale does not add, it multiplies. Every single whole step up is 10 times stronger than the step below it. So the gap between baking soda and washing soda is not three units of anything. It is about a thousand times. Think about a hand sledge and a tack hammer. Both are hammers. Both have a handle and a steel head. You would not swing a tack hammer at a concrete slab and then conclude

[2:32] that hammers do not work on concrete. But that is exactly what happens every night in kitchens across this country with a box of baking soda and a slow drain. Let me start at the bottom of that ladder and work up. Box number one is baking soda. On the label, it says sodium bicarbonate. It is the mild one. And I want to be clear that mild is not the same thing as useless because baking soda is genuinely excellent at two jobs. The first job is

[3:03] odor. Smells in a house are mostly airborne molecules and a lot of the ones we find unpleasant are on the acidic side. Sour milk, old sweat, the sharp funk from the bottom of a garbage can. Baking soda is mildly alkaline, so it neutralizes those on contact instead of just covering them with perfume. That is why an open box in the refrigerator actually does something. and why a scoop dumped into a smelly garbage disposal followed by cold water and a run of the blades will

[3:34] genuinely knock the smell down. The second job is gentle scrubbing. Baking soda crystals are soft. On the scale minologists use for hardness, it lands around 2 and 1/2, which is softer than the glaze on porcelain, softer than chrome plating, softer than a glass cooktop. So it cuts through the film without leaving those fine spiderweb scratches that a scouring powder will leave behind. I use it on porcelain sinks, on toilet bowls, on chrome

[4:05] faucets with hard water haze, and on stainless steel. It is the only abrasive I'll let near a new fixture. So why does it fail so completely on a drain? Because a drain clog in a kitchen line is almost never dirt. What you are fighting is fat. Cooking grease goes down the drain warm and liquid, hits pipe that is sitting at room temperature or colder, and hardens onto the wall of the line like candle wax in a straw. Over a year or two, it builds a ring

[4:35] inward until the opening is the size of a pencil. To break that down chemically, you have to turn the fat into soap. That reaction has a name, soponification, and it requires a strong alkali. It has been how soap gets made for about 5,000 years. A pH of 8 cannot do it. Not slowly, not overnight, not with a whole box. The chemistry simply is not there, and the vinegar makes it worse, which is the

[5:06] part that surprises people. When you pour vinegar onto baking soda, you are combining an acid and a base. They cancel each other out. What you are watching climb up your drain is carbon dioxide gas. And what is left in the pipe afterward is salty water with some bubbles in it. You took the small amount of alkalinity you had and deliberately destroyed it. The foam looks like work. It is theater. None of that is a knock on the product. A oneb box runs about a

[5:39] dollar. A 13PB bag runs four to$6 and it earns that money every week in my house. It is simply working the wrong job when you send it down a drain. So the ticket on baking soda reads like this. Hired for odor control and for scrubbing anything you are afraid to scratch. Fired from drain clearing permanently. Which leaves the middle box still sitting on the shelf unassigned. Box number two is borax. The label says

[6:10] sodium tetraarate. This one is the middle weight and it sits at a pH of about 9.2 which puts it roughly 16 times stronger than baking soda. Borax has one property the other two do not and it is the reason I keep a box in my own truck. Borax is a buffer. In plain English, a buffer is a substance that holds its position instead of getting used up the moment it meets resistance. Baking soda spends its alkalinity fast. Borax sits in a

[6:42] solution and keeps its number steady for hours. That is what makes it the right tool for anything you want to leave alone and come back to later. Where borax genuinely wins is mold and mildew. Borax contains boron, and boron interferes with the metabolism of fungus. A surface treated with borax and left to dry does not just get cleaned. It becomes a place where mold has real trouble restarting. Bleach kills what is on the surface

[7:12] today and then evaporates, which is why the black line in your shower grout comes back in 3 weeks. Borax leaves a residue behind that keeps working. The same property makes it the strongest deodorizer of the three. Not because it neutralizes harder than baking soda, but because a lot of household smell is biological. Mildew and grout, bacteria in a drain elbow, the sour note in a front-loading washer gasket. Kill the organism and the

[7:42] smell stops being produced at all. Here is the single best use for a box of borax in a home and it costs about 40 cents to do. Take your toilet and shut the supply valve behind it. Flush once so the bowl water drops. Sprinkle about a/4 cup of borax around the inside of the bowl. Get it up under the rim where you cannot see. And then take a stiff brush and paint the whole surface with a paste that forms. Leave it overnight. In

[8:12] the morning, open the valve flush and give it 30 seconds with the brush. That brown or orange ring at the water line is mineral scale with bacteria living in it. The borax loosens the scale and kills the colony at the same time and the residue it leaves behind slows the ring from coming back for a couple of months. I have taken bowls that homeowners were ready to replace and brought them back with one overnight soak and about a dollar of product.

[8:42] Quick pause. If a breakdown like that saves you one service call, my book 50 easy plumbing fixes that will save you. $10,000 does it 50 more times. 50 stepby-step repairs you can finish on a Saturday with basic tools, plus how to read a quote and spot the padding. The link is in the description. Borax also earns a spot as weekly drain maintenance. A/4 cup down a bathroom sink or a shower once a week, followed by hot tap water, will hold back the

[9:13] soap, scum, and bacterial film that make bathroom drains smell. That is prevention, and prevention is where borax lives. Two honest limits. Borax is not a grease cutter. At 9.2, it will nibble at a fresh film and it will do nothing at all to a hardened wall of kitchen fat. And borax is genuinely toxic if it is swallowed, which matters if you have small children or a dog that drinks from the toilet. Keep the box up high and

[9:44] rinse any food surface you use it on. A 65 ounce box runs $6 to 8 and lasts most households a year. So borax gets this ticket. Hired for mold, mildew, lasting odor, toilet bowls, and weekly maintenance. fired from anything involving cooked grease. Box number three is washing soda, and this is the one almost nobody has in the house. The label says sodium carbonate. It sits at a pH of about 11. That puts it about 60

[10:17] times stronger than borax and about a thousand times stronger than baking soda. And it is the only one of the three that does the thing everybody wanted baking soda to do. Washing soda soponifies grease on contact. Remember what I said about turning fat into soap. That is not a metaphor. Sodium carbonate has been used to make soap since the Egyptians were harvesting it out of dry lake beds. When

[10:47] a solution of washing soda hits the hardened grease ring inside your kitchen line, it converts the outer layer of that fat into a soap compound and soap dissolves in water. So the clog does not get pushed downstream to jam somewhere worse. It gets taken apart and carried away. Here is the treatment and the details matter more than people expect. Start at night after the last dish is done because the line needs to sit

[11:17] still. Run the hot tap for about a minute first to warm the pipe and do not skip that step because cold pipe walls make grease seize the moment your solution arrives. Now dissolve one cup of washing soda into about a half gallon of hot water in a bucket. Dissolve it first. Dumping dry powder into a standing sink packs it into a lump on top of the clog and accomplishes nothing. Pour it slowly. Take a full

[11:47] minute. You are trying to keep that solution in contact with the walls of the pipe, not blast it through the trap. Then walk away until morning. 6 to 8 hours of dwell time is where the work happens. In the morning, run hot tap water for about 2 minutes. For a moderately slow drain, one treatment usually does it. For a line that has been slowing down for a year, expect to do it two or three nights in a row. That is normal and it is still a

[12:18] fraction of what you would pay somebody else. You will know it is working before the water tells you. Grease coming loose smells faintly soapy because that is literally what it is turning into. And the film on the walls of the trap goes from tacky to slick. If you pull the trap after a second treatment, the inside will wipe clean with a rag instead of needing to be scraped. That is the difference between dissolving a clog and shoving it downstream to jam in a spot you cannot reach. Now the

[12:49] warnings and I want you to take these seriously because washing soda is strong enough to earn them. Wear gloves. At a pH of 11, this will dry out and irritate your skin and it will burn if you get it in your eyes. Do not lean over the bucket and breathe the dust while you are stirring. Never use it on aluminum. Washing soda attacks aluminum and will pit and darken it. So, keep it away from aluminum pots, aluminum window tracks,

[13:20] and older aluminum trim. Never use it in a line where a commercial drain cleaner is already sitting. If you poured a bottle of the thick blue or clear stuff down there and the water did not clear, that chemical is still in the trap. Adding anything to it is a real hazard. and that includes hot water. At that point, the honest answer is to call somebody and tell them exactly what went down the drain before they open the

[13:50] trap. Never mix it with bleach. And use hot tap water rather than a rolling boil if you have plastic drain lines. Water straight off the stove can soften a PVC joint and loosen a glued connection. and I have replaced traps for people who fixed their clog and created a leak on the same night. A 55 ounce box runs $6 to $99. A basic drain service call in most of the country runs $150 to $400 and

[14:21] hydrojetting a badly builtup kitchen line runs $400 to 800. That is the math on why this box deserves shelf space. The ticket on washing soda reads simply hired for grease, slow flow, and heavy laundry. Fired from anything delicate and anything aluminum. So now let me hand out the assignments job by job the way I would if I were standing in your laundry room. Kitchen sink draining slow or a garbage disposal

[14:51] side that gurgles. That is washing soda. One cup overnight repeat if needed. Toilet bowl with a ring at the water line or mildew under the rim. That is borax. Quarter cup. Supply valve off. Overnight soak. A smell with no drainage problem at all. Refrigerator, disposal, garbage can, carpet, gym bag. That is baking soda. And it is genuinely the best of the three at it. [15:23] running slow. This one is a split. Hair is the culprit far more often than grease. And no powder on earth dissolves hair. Pull the stopper and clear it mechanically first. And for softening hard water so your detergent stops getting wasted. Borax for whites,

[15:53] for musty towels, and for a machine that has developed a smell. Grout, shower corners, and a musty basement wall. Borax mixed into a paste, brushed on and left to dry without a heavy rinse. Porcelain, chrome, enamel, glass cooktops, and stainless. Baking soda always, because the other two are harder on a finish than they need to be. Septic systems. That is a mainline issue, and no powder is going to touch it. That is a call and it is the one situation where paying somebody is the cheap option. So the bonus, you do not actually have to buy washing soda. You can make it and you can make it tonight out of the

[17:26] cheapest box of the three. Baking soda is sodium bicarbonate. Washing soda is sodium carbonate. The only real difference between them is water and carbon dioxide locked into the crystal, and heat drives both of them off. Spread a full box of baking soda in a thin, even layer on a rimmed sheet pan. Put it in the oven at 400°. Give it about 30 minutes and stir it once at the halfway point so the layer

[17:58] underneath gets the same exposure. You will know it is finished by the way it looks and feels. Baking soda is fine, soft, and it clumps when you push it. [18:30] proof it worked. Let it cool. VIDEO: The REAL Reason S-Traps Are No Longer ALLOWED In Any Home!
Channel: Mark Silverbridge Plumbing (own) | Format: FACELESS
Selection: 5 most-viewed long-form uploads, last 24 months
Uploaded: 2026-03-18 | Duration: 19:48 | Views: 415505 | Views/normal: 756.84 (channel normal 549)
URL: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HsOr7vpQUGg | Video ID: HsOr7vpQUGg
----------------------------------------------------------------
[0:00] So, you're poking around under your kitchen sink or your bathroom vanity and you notice the drain pipe makes this weird S shape before it disappears into the floor. Maybe you've heard somewhere that's not a good thing. Or maybe a home inspector flagged it and now you're wondering what the big deal is. Here's the deal. That S trap is actually illegal in every modern plumbing code in the country and it's not just a technicality. There's a real reason they were banned

[0:30] and it has everything to do with keeping dangerous gases out of your home. I'm going to walk you through exactly what an S trap is, why it fails, how it's different from a P trap, and what you need to do if you've got one. And if you stick around to the end, I've got a bonus tip on how to tell if your traps are already dry and letting sewer gas into your house without you even realizing it. Let's get into it. Understanding how traps work is one of those fundamental

[1:00] plumbing concepts that once it clicks, a whole lot of other things start making sense. Every single drain in your house, your kitchen sink, your bathroom sink, your shower, your tub, even your toilet, has a trap built into it. And they all exist for the same reason. They hold a small amount of water that acts as a seal between your living space and the sewer line. Without that water seal, you'd have a direct open path for sewer gases to come

[1:30] right up through your drains and into your home. So, the trap itself isn't complicated. It's just a curved section of pipe that holds water. The question is how that curve is shaped and where the drain goes after it because that's where the difference between safe and dangerous comes in. Let's start with the P trap because that's the one you're supposed to have. If you look under most modern sinks, you'll see the drain come down from the basin, curve down into a U shape, and

[2:01] then go horizontally into the wall. If you look at it from the side, it kind of looks like the letter P turned on its side. That horizontal section heading into the wall is the key. When water drains through a P trap, it flows down, fills up that U-shape bend, and then exits out the back toward the wall where it connects to your drain line and your vent system. Once the water stops flowing, gravity holds a nice plug of water right there in the bottom of that curve.

[2:31] That water sits there full and level all the way up to the top of the weir, which is the overflow point of the trap. And that full water seal is what keeps sewer gas on the other side where it belongs. The reason it works so well is that there's nothing pulling that water out after the flow stops. The horizontal arm just leads to the wall, connects to a vented drain line, and the air pressure stays balanced. The water has no reason to go anywhere.

[3:01] It just sits there doing its job day after day, keeping your house smelling clean and keeping those gases out. Now, here's where things go sideways. An S trap looks similar at first glance, but instead of that horizontal arm going into the wall, the pipe curves back downward after the U-bend and goes straight into the floor. So, you've got a curve down, a curve back up, and then another curve back down, making an S shape.

[3:33] You'll usually find these in older homes, especially houses built before the 1980s or so. Back in the day, a lot of homes were roughed in with the drain coming straight up through the floor under the cabinet. So, the plumber would run the tailpiece down from the sink, form that S shape, and connect it right to the drain below. It made sense at the time with how homes were built. You'll most commonly find S traps under kitchen sinks and bathroom vanities in older houses.

[4:03] If you're looking at buying an older home, this is one of those things you absolutely want to check. Open up the cabinet doors, look under the sink, and see which direction that pipe goes after the trap. If it goes into the wall, you've got a P trap and you're in good shape. If it goes down into the floor, that's an S trap and that's a conversation you're going to want to have before you close on that house. Hey, if this is helping you out so far,

[4:34] go ahead and hit subscribe so you don't miss the next one. All right, let's keep going. So, why exactly does an S trap fail? This is the part that really matters and it comes down to one word, siphon. When water flows through an S trap, it goes down through the U-bend and then has to travel down again through that second curve into the floor. That downward path after the trap creates a siphoning effect. Think of it like when you use a hose to

[5:04] siphon gas out of a tank. Once the flow gets going in a downward direction, it wants to keep pulling. That's exactly what happens inside an S trap. As the water drains through and heads down into the floor, it creates a suction that pulls the water right out of the trap bend behind it. Instead of leaving a nice full water seal sitting in that U curve, the siphon yanks that water level way down, sometimes to the very bottom of the

[5:35] drain opening. I've seen S traps where after a single drain cycle, the water level dropped down to barely anything. Compare that to a P trap where the water holds all the way to the top of the weir. With an S trap, you might lose half or more of your water seal every time you use the sink. And here's the thing that makes it worse. Over time, as buildup collects inside the trap, soap scum, grease, hair, whatever,

[6:05] the inside diameter of that pipe gets narrower. When the opening is smaller, the siphon effect gets even stronger. So, an S trap that sort of works when it's brand new can get progressively worse as it ages. Eventually, it can siphon that trap completely dry in a single drain cycle. And once that trap is dry, you've got zero barrier between your living space and the sewer line. Nothing. Now, let's talk about what's actually in those sewer gases because this isn't

[6:36] just about a bad smell. Sewer gas is a mix of several things you do not want floating around your house. You've got hydrogen sulfide, ammonia, methane, and other byproducts of decomposing waste. Hydrogen sulfide is the one that gives it that classic rotten egg smell and in low concentrations, it can cause headaches, nausea, and irritation to your eyes and respiratory system. In higher concentrations over extended periods, it can actually be dangerous to

[7:07] your health. Methane is flammable and in the right conditions can pose an explosion risk. Now, I'm not saying your house is going to blow up because of an S trap, but these gases are genuinely not something you want to be breathing day in and day out. This is one of those situations where the plumbing code isn't just being picky for no reason. There's a real health and safety concern behind it. Plumbers exist to protect the health of the nation and the way we do that is through proper drainage, proper venting,

[7:38] and proper trap seals. When any of those three things break down, you've got a problem. Here's the other side of this that a lot of people don't think about, evaporation. Even with a P trap, if a fixture doesn't get used for a while, the water in the trap can slowly evaporate. But with a P trap, you've got a full water seal to start with, so it takes a lot longer before evaporation becomes an issue. We're talking weeks or even months

[8:08] depending on conditions. With an S trap, you're already starting from a reduced water level because of the siphon effect. So, instead of weeks, that remaining water might evaporate in a matter of days, especially in a warm, dry house. Think about a guest bathroom that nobody uses for a couple of weeks. If that bathroom has an S trap under the sink, the water in that trap was already low from the last time someone used it. Add a couple of weeks of evaporation on

[8:38] top of that and you've got a bone dry trap. Now, sewer gas has a clear path right into that bathroom and from there into the rest of your house. Ever walked into a bathroom that hasn't been used in a while and caught that nasty sewer smell? Nine times out of 10, that's a dry trap. If you've got P traps, the fix is simple. Just run the water for a few seconds in every fixture to refill the traps. Turn on the sink, run the tub for a

[9:09] moment, flush the toilet. That's all it takes to reset a P trap. But if you've got an S trap, you're refilling a trap that's going to siphon right back down the next time you use it. You're just putting a bandage on the problem. So, here's what I want you to do right now, or at least the next time you're near your kitchen or bathroom sink. Open up the cabinet, grab a flashlight if you need one, and look at the drain pipe coming down from the sink. Follow it with your eyes.

[9:40] Does it curve down into a U shape and then go horizontally into the wall? That's your P trap and you're good. Does it curve down into a U shape and then curve back down again into the floor? That's your S trap and you've got some work to do. While you're down there, look at the condition of the pipes, too. If you see old chrome or brass pipes that are corroded or white plastic pipes that are yellowed and brittle, those are signs of an older installation

[10:10] that might have other issues beyond just the trap style. Take a look at your tub and shower drains, too. Those traps are usually hidden below the floor, so you can't always see them. But, if you're getting sewer gas smell from a tub or shower in an older home, there's a chance an S-trap is lurking down there, as well. Now, let's talk about what's actually involved in replacing an S-trap with a P-trap, because this is where it gets into real plumbing work. The basic idea is straightforward. You

[10:41] need to reroute the drain, so it goes into the wall instead of into the floor. But, depending on your situation, that can range from a simple afternoon project to something that requires opening up a wall and doing some real rough-in work. If your home already has a drain connection in the wall behind the sink that's just not being used, you might be in luck. Sometimes, in older homes that have been partially updated, there's already a sanitary tee roughed into the wall at the right height, and the previous homeowner or plumber just never made the

[11:12] switch. In that case, you're looking at picking up a P-trap kit from the hardware store, a couple of slip joint nuts and washers, maybe a tailpiece extension, and connecting everything up. Most P-trap kits come with an inch and a half trap for bathroom sinks or a 1 and 1/2 to 2-in trap for kitchen sinks. Make sure you match the size to your existing drain. Measure the diameter of the pipe coming out of your wall and the tailpiece coming down from your sink before you head to the store.

[11:42] But, here's the more common scenario. If your drain only comes up through the floor, there's no wall connection at all, and that means you need to create one. This involves cutting into the wall behind the sink, tying into the existing drain line, installing a sanitary tee at the right height, and running a new horizontal arm from the tee out through the wall and into the cabinet. You'll also need to make sure there's a proper vent tied in, because a P-trap without a vent can actually create the

[12:13] same siphoning problems you're trying to fix. The vent is what keeps the air pressure balanced in the system, so the water in the trap stays put. Most codes require the vent to be within a certain distance of the trap, typically within 6 ft for an inch and a half drain, though that can vary by local code. I'll be honest with you. If you need to reroute the drain through the wall and add a vent, this is the point where a lot of homeowners are better off calling a licensed plumber. It's not that the work is impossibly

[12:44] hard, but getting the slope right on your horizontal run, tying into the vent system properly, and making sure everything meets code takes some experience. A good plumber can knock this out in a few hours, and knowing it's done right is worth the investment. If you do hire a plumber, make sure they're licensed. This isn't the kind of job you want someone learning on. Now, if your situation is on the simpler side, and you've already got a wall connection or you're comfortable doing

[13:14] the rough-in yourself, here are a few things to keep in mind during the installation. Your trap arm, that's the horizontal piece that goes from the P-trap into the wall, needs to have a slight downward slope toward the wall. You want about a quarter inch of drop per foot of run, not more, not less. Too much slope and the water outruns the waste, and you get buildup. Too little slope, and things don't drain properly. The trap arm also can't be too long.

[13:45] If the distance between your trap and the vent connection inside the wall is too far, you lose the pressure balance, and you're right back to siphoning issues. For an inch and a half pipe, keep that trap arm under 6 ft. For 2-in pipe, you've got a little more room, but don't push it. When you're tightening your slip joint connections, hand tight first, then about a quarter turn with your channel locks. Don't go crazy on them.

[14:15] Overtightening plastic slip joint nuts is one of the fastest ways to crack a fitting, and then you're making another trip to the hardware store. Let's go over some issues you might run into and how to deal with them, because even with a proper P-trap installation, things can come up. First one, you finish the install, turn on the water, and you see a slow drip at one of the slip joint connections. This is the most common issue, and it's almost always because the washer isn't

[14:45] seated right or the nut isn't tight enough. Take the connection apart, check the nylon washer, make sure it's sitting flat and facing the right direction, the beveled side goes toward the fitting you're tightening into, and reassemble. Tighten it hand tight, and then just a little more with your channel locks. If it still drips, the washer might be pinched or damaged. Swap it out with a new one. They cost almost nothing. Second one,

[15:16] you've got a P-trap installed, but you're still getting sewer gas smell. If the trap is holding water and the connections are tight, the issue is probably with the vent. If the vent is blocked or if there's no vent at all, the system can't balance pressure properly. When water drains from another fixture on the same line, it can create enough suction to pull water out of your trap, even though you've got a P-trap. This is actually how an S-trap situation

[15:46] can be created, even with a P-trap, if the installation is wrong and there's no proper venting. Check your roof vents to make sure they're not blocked by leaves, bird nests, or debris. Third, your P-trap keeps clogging. Bathroom sink traps catch a lot of hair, toothpaste, and soap buildup. If you're clearing the trap more than once or twice a year, consider putting a small mesh drain screen over your sink drain. It catches the hair before it gets into

[16:16] the trap, and it's a whole lot easier to clean a screen than to take apart a trap. For kitchen sinks, avoid putting grease down the drain. Even small amounts accumulate over time and narrow the inside of the trap. Fourth, you hear a gurgling sound when the sink drains. Gurgling almost always means a venting issue. Air is trying to get into the system to equalize pressure, and it's pulling it through the water in your trap, which is what creates that bubbling or

[16:46] gurgling noise. This is a sign that your vent might be undersized, blocked, or too far from the trap. If you just installed a new P-trap and you're hearing gurgling that wasn't there before, double-check the distance from the trap to the vent connection and make sure you're within code. Fifth, you notice water stains in the cabinet floor under the sink, but you can't find an active leak. This one's sneaky. Sometimes, slip joint connections will

[17:16] weep just a tiny amount, not enough to drip visibly, but enough to leave moisture marks over time. Feel every connection with a dry finger after running water for a minute or two. Even the slightest dampness means the connection needs to be redone. Catching it early saves you from water damage and mold down the road. Follow the steps we covered, and you should be able to handle most of what comes up. And if something is outside your comfort

[17:46] zone, there's no shame in calling a pro. That's what we're here for. All right, here's that bonus tip I mentioned at the beginning. And this is one that can save you from a sewer gas problem you didn't even know you had. If you've got fixtures in your house that don't get used very often, a guest bathroom, a basement laundry sink, a wet bar, a floor drain in the garage or utility room, those traps are slowly evaporating every single day.

[18:17] And once they dry out, you've got an open line to the sewer. Here's what I recommend. Once a month, walk through your house and run water in every fixture for about 15 to 20 seconds. Every sink, every tub, every shower. Flush every toilet. If you've got a floor drain, pour about a cup of water down it. That's all it takes to reset every trap in the house. I do this in my own home, and I recommend it to every customer.

[18:49] It takes 5 minutes, costs you nothing, and it completely prevents dry trap problems. For fixtures you really never use, like a floor drain in a storage room, you can also pour a thin layer of mineral oil down the drain after you add water. The mineral oil floats on top of the water in the trap and slows down evaporation significantly. It's an old plumber trick that works great for traps that might go months between uses. Takes about a tablespoon, and it can

[19:21] extend the life of that water seal by weeks or even months. Simple, cheap, and effective. That's the full breakdown on S-traps, P-traps, and what you need to know to keep sewer gas out of your home. If this helped you out, subscribe and hit the bell, so you don't miss the next one. Code usually requires pipes rated to at least 160 psi. So, I wanted to see what happens way beyond that. The solder joint held strong all the way

[1:31] up to around 800 lb per square inch before the copper pipe itself actually split about 2 in away from the joint. The joint was still perfect. ProPress came in impressively close, failing at around 750 psi. And the failure point was right at the fitting where the O-ring finally gave way. SharkBite held solid up to around 480 psi, which is still well above anything your home system will ever see,

[2:02] but it was clearly the first to go. The stainless steel teeth released, and the pipe shot right out. Now, does that matter for your house at 60 psi? Probably not. But it tells you something real about the engineering margin you are working with in each method. The second test that really separates these three is the torque and twist test. This one simulates what happens when somebody bumps a pipe in a wall, when a joist shifts, or when you are

[2:32] working in a tight crawl space, and accidentally put rotational stress on a connection. I locked each pipe assembly into a vise, and used a torque wrench to apply increasing rotational force to the fitting. Solder was essentially immovable. That joint is fused metal on metal, and you would destroy the pipe long before you broke that connection by twisting. ProPress also performed extremely well here. The press ring creates a mechanical crimp that resists rotation, and it took

[3:02] serious force before I saw any movement at all. SharkBite is where things get interesting. It held up fine under light to moderate torque, but once I got past about 15 ft lb, the fitting started to rotate on the pipe. It did not leak right away, but that movement tells you the O-ring seal is being stressed in a way it was not designed for. This is exactly why SharkBite fitting should always be supported with a proper bracket or clip, especially in walls or

[3:34] ceilings where things shift over time. >> [clears throat] >> The third test I put them through is the vibration endurance test, and this one matters more than most people think. I ran each assembly for 72 hours straight at a consistent frequency. Solder passed without any measurable

[4:05] change. No micro cracks, no weeping, nothing. That fused joint just absorbs vibration without complaint. ProPress also passed cleanly, though under magnification, I could see very slight burnishing on the O-ring surface. Nothing that would cause a leak in a normal lifespan, but it is worth noting. SharkBite developed a very small weep that around the 50-hour mark. We are talking about a drop every few minutes, not a spray.

[4:36] But in a closed wall over 5 or 10 years, that kind of micro leak is exactly what causes mold, rot, and the [clears throat] kind of damage you do not find until it is a $5,000 problem.
