[5:37] Fahrenheit, and then back up to room temperature each time. This simulates what happens to pipes in garages, crawl spaces, exterior walls, and cabins that go unheated in winter. Solder handled it without any issue. Copper is naturally resilient to thermal cycling, and the solder joint maintained a perfect seal every single time. ProPress surprised me a little. It also passed all 25 cycles, but on close inspection, the O-ring showed minor

[6:07] compression set, meaning it had slightly flattened from the repeated expansion and contraction. Over many years, that could theoretically reduce its sealing pressure, though the manufacturer rates these fittings for the full lifespan of the system. SharkBite actually performed better here than I expected. The push fit design allows a tiny amount of pipe movement inside the fitting, which gives it some natural tolerance for expansion. It passed all 25 cycles without leaking.

[6:37] That said, I would still insulate any SharkBite connection in a freeze-prone area because the O-ring material does stiffen in extreme cold and loses some of its elasticity. The fifth test I ran is the pull-apart tension test. This one is simple, but brutal. Think of this as what happens when a foundation settles, when thermal

[7:08] expansion pulls pipes in opposite directions, or when someone accidentally yanks on a pipe. Solder required over 200 lb of force before the copper itself tore. The joint never separated. ProPress held to about 170 lb before the crimp ring began to deform, and the pipe started to slide. SharkBite released at around 80 lb of pull force. That is still significant and well beyond what normal residential

[7:38] conditions would produce, but it is roughly half of what ProPress can handle, and less than half of solder. This is another reason why proper pipe support and strapping is absolutely critical with push fit connections. If a SharkBite fitting is doing any structural work, or if there is any tension on that line, you are asking for trouble. [8:08] I set up each connection and used a heat gun to sustain 200° Fahrenheit at the joint area for 60 minutes. This simulates pipes running near a water heater in an attic during summer, or close to a furnace or boiler. Solder was completely unbothered. The melting point of lead-free solder is around 460° Fahrenheit, so 200° is not even close to a concern. ProPress also handled it well, though

[8:39] the O-ring is rated for a maximum continuous temperature of 250°, so there is less headroom than you might assume. Here is where it gets interesting. The SharkBite O-ring is made of EPDM rubber, rated for around 200° Fahrenheit continuous. At exactly that threshold, I measured a small but real drop in seal compression. It did not leak during the test, but the margin was razor thin.

[9:09] If your SharkBite fitting is sitting near a water heater exhaust, or in a south-facing attic in Arizona, you are running right at the edge of what that O-ring can tolerate day after day, year after year. This is something almost nobody talks about, and it should factor into where you choose to use push fit connections. The seventh test is the cost and time comparison, and honestly, this is where the conversation shifts for a lot of people. I priced out a standard half-inch

[9:40] coupling for each method at current retail. A SharkBite coupling runs about $6 A ProPress coupling is around $4 to $6, but you need a press tool that starts at about $300 for a basic kit, or you can rent one for around $60 a day. A copper coupling for soldering is about $0.75 plus flux and solder bring your consumable cost to maybe $1.50 per joint and a basic torch kit costs around 30 to

[10:11] $50. Now for time. A SharkBite connection takes about 5 seconds once the pipe is cut and deburred. ProPress takes about 10 seconds per joint once the tool is set. Soldering takes about 2 to 3 minutes per joint including prep, flux, heat, and cool down. So if you are doing one or two quick repairs, SharkBite is the fastest and cheapest option with zero learning curve. If you are doing a whole house replumb or a large renovation,

[10:43] soldering gives you the lowest material cost by a wide margin and ProPress sits right in the middle offering professional speed with professional-grade reliability. But that tool investment only makes sense if you are doing a lot of joints. The eighth and final test is the long-term reliability verdict and this is where I pull everything together. Across all seven tests, solder won in every single category except speed and ease of use.

[11:14] It is the strongest, most heat resistant, most vibration proof, and cheapest per joint connection method available. If you have the skill and the time, solder is still king. ProPress came in as the clear second place overall. And honestly, it is what most professional plumbers are switching to for good reason. The reliability is very close to solder, the speed is dramatically faster, and the consistency is excellent because the

[11:46] tool removes human error from the equation. You get the same crimp every single time. SharkBite is not a bad product. Let me be clear about that. It passed every test within normal residential operating ranges. But it had the smallest safety margins across the board. Where I would use SharkBite without hesitation is for accessible repairs, shutoff valves, temporary fixes, and situations where you need a connection fast and you can get to it later if

[12:18] anything ever goes wrong. Where I would not use it is behind finished walls, in ceiling cavities, or anywhere you cannot visually inspect it on a regular basis. All right. Here is the bonus tip the pros actually use. If you do install SharkBite fittings, there is a simple trick that dramatically improves their reliability and almost nobody does it. After you push the pipe into the fitting, take a permanent marker and draw a line

[12:48] on the pipe right where it meets the fitting edge. That mark becomes your visual indicator. If the fitting ever starts to creep or the pipe shifts even a fraction, you will see the mark move away from the edge of the fitting. Professional plumbers who use push-fit connections in commercial settings do this as standard practice. It takes 2 seconds and it gives you an early warning system that can save you thousands in water damage. One line from a marker can be the

[13:18] difference between catching a problem and coming home to a flooded basement. That wraps it up. If this was useful, you will love the full book. 50 step-by-step plumbing fixes with diagrams all in one guide. Link is below. Hit subscribe so you do not miss the next video. Thanks for watching.

VIDEO: Tankless vs Tank Water Heater
Channel: Mark Silverbridge Plumbing (own) | Format: FACELESS
Selection: 5 most-viewed long-form uploads, last 24 months
Uploaded: 2026-04-04 | Duration: 15:43 | Views: 279335 | Views/normal: 508.81 (channel normal 549)
URL: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=du--FqUoCHI | Video ID: du--FqUoCHI
----------------------------------------------------------------
[0:00] So, your water heater is on its last legs. Or maybe you are building new and trying to figure out which direction to go. And every single person you talk to has a different opinion. Your neighbor swears by his tankless unit. Your brother-in-law says tank style is the only way to go. And then you go online and it gets even worse because half the articles are written by companies trying to sell you something. Well, I have been a plumber for over 35 years.

[0:30] And I have personally installed hundreds of both tank and tankless water heaters in every kind of home you can imagine. Today, I am going to give you the honest, no-nonsense comparison that nobody else will. Including the real cost numbers, the actual energy savings, and the stuff the sales brochures conveniently leave out. And stick around to the end because I am going to tell you about a third option that most people have never even heard

[1:00] of that might beat both of these. Let us get into it. This is one of the biggest decisions you will make for your home's plumbing system. And it is not cheap either way. The wrong choice can cost you thousands over the life of the unit. So, let us break this down piece by piece starting with how these two systems actually work. Because understanding that is the foundation for everything else. All right, let us start simple. A

[1:30] traditional tank water heater is basically a big insulated cylinder, usually 40 or 50 gallons for most homes, that keeps water hot and ready to go 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. There is a burner at the bottom if it is gas. Or heating elements inside if it is electric. And they kick on whenever the water temperature drops below the thermostat setting. Think of it like a big thermos that constantly reheats itself. The upside is you have got a reservoir

[2:01] of hot water sitting there ready the moment you turn on a faucet. The downside is you are paying to keep that water hot even at 3:00 in the morning when nobody is using it. That is what the industry calls standby heat loss and it is real. You can lose anywhere from 15 to 25% of your energy just keeping that tank warm. Now, a tankless unit works completely differently. There is no storage tank at all. When you open a hot water faucet, cold

[2:32] water flows through the unit, hits a high-powered burner or electric element, and gets heated instantly as it passes through. The moment you turn off the faucet, the unit shuts down. No standby loss. No keeping 50 gallons hot while you are at work all day. Sounds perfect, right? Well, hold on because there is a lot more to this story. Let us talk about what these things actually cost to buy and install.

[3:02] Because this is where most people get their first big surprise. Here is the honest truth on pricing. A standard 40 or 50 gallon tank water heater, gas-fired, is going to run you somewhere between $400 and $800 for the unit itself. A decent mid-range model, which is what I put in most homes, sits right around 550 to 650. Installation on a straight swap, meaning you are replacing an old tank with a new

[3:33] one in the same spot, typically runs $300 to $600 in labor depending on your area. So, all in you are looking at roughly $800 to $1,400 for most tank installations. Now, here is where tankless gets interesting. The unit itself for a whole-house gas tankless system runs anywhere from $800 to $1,500. But the unit cost is not what gets you. It is the installation. If your home has never had a tankless

[4:04] unit before, you are likely looking at upgrading your gas line from a 3/4 inch to a 1-inch line because these units demand a lot more gas volume. You may need a new dedicated vent run and it cannot be the old B vent your tank used. Most tankless units require a stainless steel category 3 or a concentric vent kit. You might also need an electrical outlet installed nearby for the control board.

[4:34] When you add all of that up, a first-time tankless installation commonly runs between $2,500 and $4,500 total. I have done some that hit 5,000 in older homes that needed significant gas line rework. That is a big difference from the tank price and it is the number one thing people do not account for when they start shopping. By the way, if you are finding this helpful, you should know this is just a taste of what I have put into my book, 50 Easy Plumbing Fixes That Will Save

[5:06] You $10,000. It covers everything from leaky faucets to running toilets step-by-step with diagrams. You can grab your copy by scanning the QR code on screen right now. Or just use the first link in the description below. All right, let us keep going. So, the big question everyone wants answered is whether a tankless unit will save enough on monthly bills to justify that higher upfront cost. Let me give you the real numbers from what I have seen.

[5:37] The Department of Energy estimates that tankless water heaters are about 24 to 34% more energy efficient for homes that use 41 gallons or less per day. For homes using a lot more, around 86 gallons a day, the savings drops to about 8 to 14%. In real dollar terms, most families I work with save somewhere between 80 and 150 dollars per year on gas bills after switching to tankless. Now, let me do that math for you.

[6:09] If your tankless installation cost you $3,000 more than a tank would have and you are saving $100 a year, that is a 30-year payback period. That is not great. If you are saving 150 a year, you are looking at 20 years to break even. And here is what the brochures never mention. Energy prices fluctuate. Your usage changes as kids move out or move in. And the unit needs maintenance that costs money, too.

[6:39] The savings are real, but they are not as dramatic as most people expect. Where tankless really shines on efficiency is in homes where hot water usage is spread out throughout the day rather than concentrated in a morning rush. If you and your spouse both shower, run the dishwasher, and do laundry all within the same 2-hour window, a tankless unit is working incredibly hard during that burst and sitting idle the rest of the day, which narrows the

[7:09] efficiency gap considerably. Let us talk about how long these things last cuz this is where the equation starts to shift. A standard tank water heater, well maintained, gives you about 8 to 12 years. I have seen some go 15, but that is the exception, not the rule. And most people do not maintain them at all, which means you are realistically looking at 8 to 10 years. A tankless unit, properly maintained,

[7:39] can last 20 years or more. I have pulled out tankless units that were 18, 19 years old and still running strong. That means over a 30-year period you are buying two or maybe three tank heaters versus one, possibly two tankless units. When you factor in the cost of those additional tank replacements, the total cost of ownership gap closes significantly. Now, here is the maintenance piece that catches people off guard. Tankless units need to be descaled and

[8:10] flushed at least once a year, especially if you have hard water. If you skip this, mineral buildup will choke that heat exchanger and kill your unit years early. A professional flush runs about $100 to $150. Or you can buy a descaling kit for about 40 bucks and do it yourself in about an hour. Tank heaters need their anode rod checked every 3 to 4 years and the tank flushed annually, too, though almost nobody does it.

[8:40] An anode rod is $20 and takes about 15 minutes to swap. The point is both systems need attention. Neither one is set it and forget it. Now, let us get into everyday performance because this is where you actually live with your decision. A tank water heater gives you a simple, predictable experience. You turn on the hot tap and hot water comes out almost immediately because it is already sitting there in the tank hot

[9:11] and ready. The limitation is the capacity. If you have a 50-gallon tank and your family burns through 50 gallons in a morning rush, you are taking a cold shower. Recovery time on a gas tank is roughly 30 to 40 minutes to reheat a full tank. Tankless units advertise unlimited hot water and technically that is true. As long as water is flowing through the unit, it will keep heating it. But there is a catch called flow rate.

[9:43] A typical whole-house gas tankless unit puts out somewhere between 8 and 10 gallons per minute in warm climates. But in cold climates where the incoming water temperature is 40 or 45 degrees, that drops to 4 or 5 gallons per minute. That means if someone is showering at two gallons per minute and someone else starts the dishwasher at 1.5 gallons per minute and then a third person turns on a bathroom faucet, you might exceed the unit's capacity and

[10:13] everyone gets lukewarm water. The other thing nobody warns you about is the cold water sandwich. You turn on the hot tap. You get a short burst of hot water that was sitting in the pipes. Then a slug of cold water hits before the tankless unit fires up and sends fresh hot water through. It is not a deal breaker, but it catches people off guard and it is annoying. Let us go over some common problems you might run into with each type and how to

[10:44] deal with them. On the tank side, the number one issue I see is a leaking pressure relief valve. You will notice water dripping from that little pipe running down the side of your tank. Usually, this means the valve is doing its job because pressure or temperature is too high inside the tank or the valve itself has gone bad. Check your thermostat setting first. It should be at 120°. If that is fine, replace the valve,

[11:16] which is about 15 to 20 dollars and a 15-minute job. Second common tank problem is rusty or discolored hot water. This almost always means your anode rod is spent and the tank itself is starting to corrode inside. If you catch it early, replace the anode rod and flush the tank. If the tank is already rusting through, it is time for a new heater. Third issue is strange popping or rumbling noises from the tank.

[11:47] That is sediment buildup on the bottom getting superheated. Flush the tank thoroughly and it usually resolves it. On the tankless side, the most common issue I deal with is error codes related to ignition failure. This usually means the gas supply is inadequate, the vent is partially blocked, or the igniter is dirty. Check your gas valve first. Make sure it is fully open and inspect the vent termination outside for any debris or bird nests.

[12:17] Second big tankless problem is fluctuating water temperature. This is often caused by flow rates that are too low to trigger the unit or too high for it to keep up. Most tankless units have a minimum flow rate of around half a gallon per minute to activate. Low flow fixtures can sometimes fall below that threshold. Third is mineral scale buildup causing reduced performance. If your hot water output seems weaker than it used to be, descale the heat exchanger.

[12:47] In hard water areas, I tell my customers to flush every 6 months instead of once a year. Prevention on both sides comes down to the same thing. Annual maintenance is not optional if you want these systems to last. So, which one is right for your home? Here is my honest framework after hundreds of installs. Go with a tank water heater if you are on a tighter budget upfront. Your home already has a tank setup with proper gas and venting. Your household has predictable hot water

[13:17] usage concentrated in a short window. Or you plan to sell the home within the next 5 to 7 years and do not need to recoup a long payback period. Go with tankless if you plan to stay in your home long-term, 15 years or more. Your household uses hot water spread throughout the day. You have the budget for the higher upfront installation cost. You are in a moderate climate where incoming water temperatures are not extremely cold.

[13:48] Or you value the space saving since tankless units mount on a wall and free up that entire closet or utility area. There is no universally right answer. It depends on your house, your family, your budget, and how long you plan to stay. All right, here is that bonus tip I promised you. There is a third option that most homeowners have never even considered and it is called a hybrid heat pump water heater. These units look like a regular tank

[14:19] heater, but they have a heat pump on top that pulls warmth from the surrounding air to heat the water. Kind of like a refrigerator running in reverse. They use about 60% less energy than a standard electric tank heater. The upfront cost sits between 1,200 and 2,000 dollars for the unit plus installation, which puts them right between a tank and tankless in total price. The real kicker is the rebates. Depending on your state,

[14:49] federal tax credits and local utility rebates can knock 700 to over a thousand dollars off that price. I have been installing more and more of these over the past few years and the customer satisfaction has been outstanding. They do need to be installed in a space that stays between about 40 and 90° Fahrenheit with at least a thousand cubic feet of air space around them. So, a garage or large utility room works great, but a small closet does not.

[15:19] If you have the right space for one, seriously look into it. It might be the best value option out of all three. That wraps it up. VIDEO: 10 Water Heater Mistakes That Are Costing You THOUSANDS!
Channel: Mark Silverbridge Plumbing (own) | Format: FACELESS
Selection: 5 most-viewed long-form uploads, last 24 months
Uploaded: 2026-03-08 | Duration: 17:02 | Views: 183819 | Views/normal: 334.83 (channel normal 549)
URL: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VZo8y6AplVQ | Video ID: VZo8y6AplVQ
----------------------------------------------------------------
[0:00] Most homeowners never think about their water heater until something goes wrong. By then, you're looking at hundreds or even thousands of dollars in repairs you could have avoided. With over 25 years in the trades, I've seen the same mistakes come up again and again. And today, I'm walking you through 10 of them. Stick around because number eight catches almost everyone off guard. And the bonus tip at the end could save your tank years of wear. The first mistake on this list is

[0:31] ignoring the anode rod. And it is honestly the single biggest reason water heaters fail before their time. The anode rod is a long metal rod, usually made of magnesium or aluminum, that runs down the center of your tank. Its entire job is to corrode so your tank does not. It is called a sacrificial rod for exactly that reason. The problem is that most homeowners have never heard of it, let alone checked it.

[1:02] Once that rod is fully corroded, there is nothing left protecting the steel lining of your tank, and rust sets in fast. On most standard tank water heaters, the anode rod is located on top of the unit, either under a plastic cap or directly exposed. You will need a 1 and 1/16 inch socket and a breaker bar cuz these are almost always on tight. Before you pull it, shut off the cold water supply and release some pressure by opening a hot water faucet somewhere in the house.

[1:32] If the rod looks thin, flaky, or is coated heavily in calcium build-up, replace it. A new anode rod costs between 20 and 40 dollars at any hardware store. Replacing it every 3 to 5 years can add a decade to your tank's life. Ignoring it can cost you 500 to 1500 dollars in a full tank replacement. That is one of the easiest trade-offs in home maintenance. The second mistake that quietly drains money every single month is setting the

[2:03] temperature too high or too low on your water heater. Most units ship from the factory set somewhere between 120 and 1 140° F, but a lot of homeowners never touch the dial after installation. That might sound fine, but it is worth knowing exactly what you are dealing with. Setting the temperature too high, above 140° F, increases your risk of scalding, especially for children and older

[2:33] adults. It also forces your heater to work harder than necessary, which shows up on your energy bill every month. Setting it too low, below 120° F, creates a different problem. Water sitting in the tank at lower temperatures can become a breeding ground for Legionella bacteria, which causes Legionnaires' disease. That is not a scare tactic. It is a documented public health risk. The sweet spot recommended by most

[3:03] plumbing codes and the Department of Energy is 120° F for standard households. If you have someone immunocompromised at home or a dishwasher without an internal heater, you may want to bump it slightly higher. The adjustment itself takes less than 5 minutes. On gas units, there is typically a dial near the burner assembly. On electric units, you will need to remove the access panel and adjust the thermostat directly.

[3:33] A simple fix that most people overlook entirely. The third mistake is one that genuinely surprises people when I bring it up, and that is skipping the pressure relief valve test. This valve, often called the TPR valve, or temperature pressure relief valve, is your water heater's last line of defense against a catastrophic failure. If pressure or temperature inside the tank gets too high, this valve is supposed to open and release it.

[4:04] The problem is that these valves can seize up from mineral deposits and corrosion over time, meaning they will not function when you actually need them. Testing it is straightforward. There is a small lever on the valve, usually located on the upper side of the tank. Place a bucket under the discharge pipe first, then lift the lever briefly for about 3 seconds and release it. You should hear a rush of water or steam, and then it should snap shut cleanly. If it drips continuously after the test,

[4:36] the valve is worn and needs replacing. If nothing comes out at all, the valve is likely seized and must be replaced immediately. A replacement TPR valve costs between $15 and $30 and installs with basic tools. Neglecting this test is not just expensive, it is genuinely dangerous. A failed TPR valve under extreme pressure has caused tanks to rupture with enough force to cause serious structural

[5:06] damage. Test it once a year. It takes less than 2 minutes and gives you real peace of mind. Most homeowners have never done it once. Well, if this is helping you out, do me a favor and hit that like button and subscribe. It helps get this video in front of more people who could use the info. All right, let's keep going. The fourth mistake on this list is one that affects nearly every home with hard water, and that is failing to flush sediment from the tank.

[5:37] Over time, minerals like calcium and magnesium naturally settle at the bottom of your water heater tank. This layer of sediment acts as an insulating barrier between the burner and the water, which means your unit has to work harder and run longer to heat the same amount of water. You might notice it as a popping or rumbling sound coming from the tank. That noise is steam bubbling up through the sediment layer. Left unchecked, heavy sediment build-up

[6:07] can reduce the efficiency of your water heater significantly, shortening its lifespan and inflating your energy bills month after month. In extreme cases, it can cause the bottom of the tank to overheat and fail entirely. Flushing the tank is something most homeowners can handle themselves. Attach a garden hose to the drain valve at the bottom of the unit, run it to a floor drain or outside. Shut off the cold water inlet and open the valve. Let it drain until the water runs clear.

[6:39] On older units with a plastic drain valve, be gentle. These valves can be brittle and sometimes fail during the process. If yours looks worn, have a replacement valve on hand before you start. Aim to flush your tank once a year. It takes about 30 minutes and directly protects your investment. The fifth mistake is one where the consequences go well beyond money, and that is improper venting on gas water heaters. When a gas water heater burns fuel, it

[7:10] produces combustion gases, including carbon monoxide. Those gases need a clear, properly sized path to exit your home. Vent pipe that is the wrong diameter, incorrectly pitched, or connected with the wrong materials can cause those gases to backdraft directly into your living space. This is not a theoretical risk. Carbon monoxide is colorless and odorless, and exposure can be fatal before you realize anything is wrong.

[7:41] The most common DIY errors here involve using flexible aluminum dryer vent instead of rigid metal vent pipe, pitching the vent pipe downward instead of maintaining a consistent upward slope toward the flue, and failing to maintain proper clearances from combustible materials. For most residential gas water heaters, you want single wall metal vent pipe with a minimum upward pitch of 1/4 inch per foot toward the draft hood. Double wall type B vent is required in

[8:12] certain applications, particularly when passing through walls or ceilings. Local codes vary, so always check before you start. If you have any doubt about your current venting setup, call a licensed plumber or HVAC technician for an inspection. The cost of a professional venting correction is a fraction of what carbon monoxide exposure can cost a family. The sixth mistake that trips up a lot of confident DIYers is using the wrong pipe

[8:42] materials for water heater connections. Specifically, the problem is connecting copper pipe directly to a galvanized steel nipple on the water heater without using a dielectric union in between. When two dissimilar metals come into direct contact in the presence of water, you get galvanic corrosion. Over time, that connection will corrode, leak, and potentially contaminate your water supply with rust and scale. The fix is simple and inexpensive.

[9:12] Dielectric unions are fittings specifically designed to separate dissimilar metals with a plastic or rubber insulating sleeve. They cost a few dollars each and are available at any plumbing supply or hardware store. Always use them when transitioning from copper to galvanized or steel connections at the tank. Another common version of this mistake involves using push-to-connect fittings or shark bite style connectors right at the water heater in high heat zones.

[9:42] Most push-to-connect fittings are rated for use with water heaters, but always verify the temperature rating on the packaging before you install them. Fittings rated for standard potable water lines may not hold up to the sustained heat at the tank connections. Spend the extra few minutes to use the right fittings and you will avoid a frustrating leak down the road. The seventh mistake is one that has become increasingly relevant as more homes are built with closed plumbing systems, and that is failing to install an

[10:12] expansion tank. In an open plumbing system, when water heats up and expands, that extra pressure has somewhere to go back toward the municipal supply. But most modern homes have a pressure reducing valve or a check valve installed, which creates what is called a closed system. In a closed system, there is nowhere for that thermal expansion to go. So, pressure builds inside the tank every time the water heats up.

[10:42] Over time, this repeated pressure cycling hammers your TPR valve, stresses your tank fittings, and can dramatically shorten the life of the unit. Many municipalities now require an expansion tank by code on any closed system installation. The tank itself is a small vessel, typically 2 to 4 gallons filled with air and separated by a rubber bladder. It absorbs the pressure spike every time your water heater fires.

[11:13] Installation is straightforward. The expansion tank is installed on the cold water supply line entering the heater, usually with a simple T fitting. Make sure the pre-charge pressure in the tank matches your home's water pressure before installing. You can check the tank's air charge with a standard tire pressure gauge on the valve stem. An expansion tank costs between $30 and $70 and can prevent hundreds of dollars in repeated valve and fitting failures. The eighth mistake is one that nearly everyone is guilty of at some point, and

[11:45] that is delaying a water heater replacement past the unit's usable lifespan. Most standard tank water heaters are designed to last between 8 and 12 years. Some will push past that with good maintenance, but they are running on borrowed time. The problem is that homeowners tend to wait for a visible failure, meaning a leak or a complete loss of hot water before they act. By that point, you are often dealing with an emergency replacement, and emergency replacements cost

[12:16] significantly more. You lose the ability to shop around, compare prices, or schedule a convenient installation window. You are now calling whoever can come out fastest and paying whatever they charge. That premium can easily add $500 to $1,000 to the total cost compared to a planned replacement. Check the age of your unit right now. The manufacture date is encoded in the serial number, and every major brand uses a slightly different format.

[12:47] A quick search of your brand serial number decoder will tell you exactly how old the unit is. If you are pushing past 10 years, start budgeting and planning now. A proactive replacement on your schedule is always cheaper and less stressful than a flooded utility room at midnight. The ninth mistake is one that some experienced DIYers still make, and that is doing gas line work without pulling a permit. It is tempting to skip the permit process to save time and money,

[13:19] but gas line modifications without an inspection create serious problems that can follow you for years. First, there is the obvious safety issue. Gas line connections require proper materials, correct fittings, and a pressure test to confirm there are no leaks before the system goes live. Without an inspection, a small mistake can go undetected until it becomes a catastrophe. Second, unpermitted gas work can void your homeowner's insurance policy.

[13:51] If a fire or explosion is traced back to unpermitted work, your insurer has grounds to deny the claim entirely. Third, when you sell your home, unpermitted work can surface during the buyer's inspection and either kill the deal or force you to remediate it at your own expense. The permit process for water heater replacement or gas line work varies by jurisdiction, but in most areas, it is straightforward. Pull the permit, do the work, schedule

[14:21] the inspection. It adds a small amount of time and a modest fee, but it protects your investment and your family. The 10th and final mistake on this list is one of the easiest and cheapest to fix, and that is not insulating your hot water pipes. Every foot of uninsulated hot water pipe running through your walls, crawl space, or basement is bleeding heat before that water ever reaches your faucet. You end up running the tap longer to get

[14:52] hot water, which wastes both water and energy. In colder climates, uninsulated pipes near exterior walls or in unconditioned spaces are also at risk of freezing. Foam pipe insulation, sometimes called pipe lagging, is available at any hardware store and costs almost nothing. It comes in standard sizes to fit half inch and 3/4 inch pipe, the two most common residential sizes. It is pre-slit down one

[15:22] so it snaps directly onto the pipe. No special tools required. Start with the first 3 to 4 ft of pipe leaving the water heater, then work outward along any runs in unconditioned spaces. This is one of those rare fixes where the payoff is immediate. Shorter wait times for hot water, lower energy bills, and better freeze protection in a single afternoon of work for under $20 in materials. All right, here is the bonus tip the

[15:52] pros actually use and most homeowners never hear about. When you replace your anode rod, do not just swap it and forget it. Drop a small handful of water softener pellets, the standard sodium chloride type, directly into the tank through the anode rod port before you thread the new rod in. This trick helps break up existing scale deposits coating the inside of the tank and slightly softens the water contacting the new rod, which slows its corrosion rate. You get better protection and a longer

[16:23] interval between rod changes. It costs almost nothing since you only need a small amount and it takes 30 seconds to do. Professional plumbers who service water heaters regularly use this technique to extend the life of tanks that are already showing early signs of sediment buildup. Combined with an annual flush, it is one of the most effective low-cost maintenance moves you can make to keep your water heater running efficiently for as long as possible. Thanks for watching the whole thing.

[16:54] If you got something out of this, leave a like. Subscribe for more and check out the other videos on the channel.

[0:00] That bleach tablet in your toilet tank is destroying a $5 part that keeps the whole thing working. [0:31] why the bleach has to go. Number one, that tablet is eating your toilet from the inside. Lift the lid off the tank and look at the flapper down at the bottom, the soft rubber disc that lifts when you press the handle and drops back to seal the tank. Same goes for the gasket under the flush valve and the seals inside the fill valve, the tower over in the corner that refills the tank after every flush. That damage shows up in months, and the tablet keeps working on those parts whether anybody flushes or not. And here's the part people skip. A warped flapper does not announce itself. It just stops sealing clean, and the tank starts leaking water down into the bowl in a quiet trickle. That is the

[1:33] running toilet you hear at 2:00 in the morning, the phantom flush, the hiss that never quits, and it runs up the water bill month after month. Plenty of people call a plumber over it because a toilet that will not stop running sounds like a real problem. Nine times out of 10, it is a $5 flapper. One part, one wet hand, 10 minutes, done. I've pulled tablets out of tanks where every piece of rubber in there had gone hard as a hockey puck and the companies that make those rubber parts even print warnings

[2:03] about tank tablets right on the packaging in the fine print nobody reads. If you want a cleaner bowl, clean the bowl. Keep the chemicals out of the tank where all the working parts live. Now, the reason that tablet went in there in the first place is usually a stain that bleach cannot even touch. Number two, bleach has never once removed a mineral ring. That chalky ring at the waterline, the one that creeps back no matter how hard you scrub, is mineral, plain and simple. Calcium and lime left behind by your own water and

[2:33] baked onto the porcelain one flush at a time. Bleach is a disinfectant. It kills and it whitens and it does nothing at all to mineral scale because scale is basically rock and rock does not care about bleach. A mild acid is what takes it apart. The old fix is citric acid, the same stuff that makes lemons sour, sold as a plain powder for a few dollars a jar in the canning aisle. Shut the water off at the little valve on the wall behind the toilet. Flush once to drop the bowl level, pour in a good

[3:03] scoop and let it sit for a couple of hours. The ring softens and wipes away with a brush like it was never there. Plain white vinegar does the same job if you give it an overnight soak. Between you and me, I've seen bowls scrubbed raw over that ring year after year when one quiet afternoon with a jar of powder would have ended it for good. All that scrubbing never stood a chance because the chemistry was wrong. And once you know scale only answers to acid, you will start spotting it all over the house, on the shower head, around the

[3:34] faucet, inside the kettle and the same cheap jar handles every bit of it. But before you get brave and mix a stronger bowl cleaner into the fight, there is one combination under that sink that can flat out hurt you. Number three, bleach plus the wrong cleaner makes poison gas. This is the one part of this video I need you to remember even if you forget all the rest. Chlorine bleach mixed with any acid releases chlorine gas, the same family of gas they used in the First World War. And plenty of bowl cleaners

[4:04] are acid-based because acid is what cuts mineral scale, like we just covered. So, the fellow who pours bleach in the bowl, watches the ring shrug it off, and dumps a shot of lime remover on top, has just gassed himself at knee height in the smallest room in the house. Bleach and ammonia are the same story. That pair makes chloramine gas, and ammonia hides in a lot of glass cleaners. So, read the back label on anything before it goes in the same bowl. The rule is simple, and it is absolute. One cleaner in the bowl

[4:34] at a time, and flush well before you ever switch products. If you ever catch that sharp swimming pool smell biting at your throat, get out of the bathroom, shut the door behind you, and let the room air out before you go back in. Now, here is the good news. You do not need bleach in there at all. Plain hydrogen peroxide, the brown bottle from the first aid shelf, handles the freshening side of the job just fine. Pour in a cup, let it sit half an hour, scrub, and flush. Peroxide and citric acid between

[5:04] them cover everything you were buying bleach for, without putting chlorine anywhere near those rubber parts. Which brings us to the weak flush, the one you have been blaming on the toilet itself. The real culprit sits less than an inch from where you scrub every week. And in a minute, I will put your finger right on it. First, while the bowl is clean, one quick thing that belongs right here. None of these fixes started with me. They have been handed around the trade for generations, and I gathered the drain side into a digital book called

[5:34] 101 Ways to Unclog Any Drain. It covers every drain in the house, zone by zone. Kitchen sink and disposal, bathroom sink, tub and shower, the toilet, laundry and floor drains out to the main line with fixes built around ordinary stuff already in your cabinet. So, the morning a drain backs up, the calm answer is already on your phone. There is a QR code on your screen right now. Point your phone camera at it and tap the link that pops up, or use the

[6:05] same link on the first line of the description box under this video. No hurry on any of it. The book keeps. Now, about that lazy flush. Number four, the weak flush is usually not the toilet's fault. Run a finger up under the rim of the bowl, along the underside you never see. You will feel a ring of small holes. Those are the rim jets. The little nozzles that fire tank water down the walls of the bowl every time you flush, and they are what gives a flush its push. In a house with hard water,

[6:35] those holes slowly close up with mineral scale, the same crust from the waterline ring. Only now, it is choking the plumbing instead of staining it. Each jet narrows a little more every year. The swirl gets lazier, and one day you are holding the handle down while the water just mopes around the bowl. Most folks never check this. They decide the toilet is worn out, and some of them go price a brand new one over what is, I promise you, a clogged shower head problem wearing a porcelain disguise.

[7:05] Here is the fix, and it costs almost nothing. Shut off the supply valve, flush the tank empty, and feed white vinegar or that citric acid mix down the overflow tube, the open pipe standing up in the middle of the tank, so it fills the rim channel from the inside. Let it sit a couple of hours. Then, take a piece of stiff wire, a straightened coat hanger, even a small Allen wrench, and ream each jet clean. Scrub under the rim, turn the water back on, and flush. The difference will make you laugh out loud. I've

[7:36] watched a tired old bowl come back to a full hard swirl in one afternoon with nothing but vinegar and a coat hanger in a house where somebody had already been pricing out a replacement. And those rim jets have a big brother down at the bottom of the bowl doing most of the heavy lifting that almost nobody has ever looked at on purpose. Number five, the hole at the bottom of the bowl does the real work. Look down into the bowl, right at the bottom toward the front. There is one larger hole down there aimed back at the drain opening. That is

[8:06] the siphon jet and in plain terms, it is the muscle of the whole flush. The rim jets rinse the walls, but that bottom jet fires a slug of water straight into the trapway, the S-shaped tunnel cast inside the porcelain. And that jolt is what starts the siphon, the suction that actually pulls everything down and away. When people say a toilet has a strong flush, they are talking about that one hole whether they know it or not. And because it sits underwater its whole life, it scales up even worse than the

[8:36] rim does. A crusted siphon jet is the difference between one clean pull and that weak double flush habit half the country has gotten used to. And every one of those second flushes is a full tank of water spent on a 10-minute cleaning job nobody did. While your vinegar or citric soap is sitting in the bowl anyway, take the brush or a gloved finger and work that bottom hole clean like you mean it. A small mirror helps you see what you are doing if the angle fights you. I'll tell you, I've cleared jets so crusted over that the owners had

[9:06] lived with a sad flush for years and called it normal. 10 minutes with a wire, not one new part, nothing from a store, and that bathroom worked differently every single day afterward. Now put the bowl brush down and lift the lid off the tank because the next one is a free adjustment hiding in plain water. Number six, the water level in your tank has a right answer. With the lid off, watch where the water settles after a flush. The right level is about an inch below the top of the overflow tube, the same open pipe we poured the vinegar

[9:36] down a minute ago. Plenty of tanks even have a thin line scratched into the porcelain marked water line and almost nobody has ever read it. Too low and the bowl never gets its full charge of water. So, you get a weak flush out of a perfectly healthy toilet. Too high and water dribbles over the top of that tube around the clock straight down the drain, which is the other big cause of the running toilet sound and it bills you for every drop. The adjustment is free and takes 2 minutes. On most modern fill valves, there is a plastic screw or

[10:07] a slide clip riding along the float, the part that rises with the water and tells the valve when to shut off. Turn the screw or pinch and slide the clip down for less water, up for more. Then flush and watch where it settles. On the old style with the long brass arm and the round ball on the end, you just bend the arm gently in the direction you want. That is the whole job. A screwdriver, 2 minutes, and a tank filling to the line it was engineered for. Check it once a year when you test your smoke detectors and half the mystery problems in this

[10:38] video never get the chance to start. Everything so far has been about the toilet you already own. These next two are about the day you go buy one and the first will save you from wrestling porcelain back into a truck. Number seven, measure the rough in before you ever shop for a toilet. Every toilet is built around one measurement called the rough in, which is just the distance from the finished wall behind the toilet to the center of the drain pipe under it. You can find it without pulling a thing. Run a tape measure from the wall,

[11:08] the wall itself, not the baseboard, out to the center of one of the two little bolt caps at the base, the caps covering the bolts that hold the toilet down. 12 in is the standard in most American homes. Older houses sometimes run 10 and a few run 14. Why it matters is simple. Buy a 12-in toilet for a 10-in rough in and it physically will not sit right because the tank hits the wall before the bolts line up. Buy a 10 for a 12 and it sits a sad gap off the wall looking like it wandered into the room by

[11:38] accident. Either way, you are wrestling a heavy chunk of porcelain back into the truck, paying a restocking fee, and burning the Saturday you set aside to install it. I've seen brand new toilets sitting in garages for months because nobody checked one number a tape measure gives up in 30 seconds. Write it in your phone, wall to bolt cap, and while you are down there, note the bowl shape. Two, round or the longer oval because the seat and the knee room in front depend on it. And while we are talking toilet shopping, let me knock down the

[12:08] rumor that keeps people nursing a 40-year-old water hog. Then, stick with me for number nine because it ends the most expensive misunderstanding in this whole video. Number eight, the new low-flow toilets flush better than your old one. I know what some of you are thinking because the first wave of low-flow toilets back in the '90s was awful and I will not argue otherwise. Those early units took the old bowl design and starved it of water. And the result? Clogged if you looked at it wrong. People flushed twice, grumbled plenty, and swore off the whole idea for

[12:40] life. That was 30 years ago and the engineering caught up. A good modern toilet moves 1.28 gallons per flush and clears the bowl as well as the old 3 and 1/2 gallon units ever did because the whole design changed around the smaller tank. Wider trapways glazed slick inside so nothing hangs up. Taller tanks for more drop and a siphon jet, that bottom hole we just cleaned, aimed like it means business. The water per flush dropped by almost 2/3 and the performance came all the way back. Now,

[13:10] run the arithmetic with me. The toilet is the biggest water user inside most homes. So, if yours dates from the era of wood paneling, every single flush is sending close to three times the water down the drain that a modern one would. And your water bill has been carrying that for decades without you ever noticing. A new unit pays you back flush by flush and then keeps right on paying. So, when number seven sends you shopping with that rough-in written down, buy modern with a clear conscience because the double flush days are over. Which

[13:41] brings us to the last one, the wax ring and the repair bill that never needed to exist. Number nine, a wax ring is good for decades unless the toilet rocks. Under the base of every toilet sits a ring of plain wax squashed between the porcelain and the closet flange, the pipe fitting bolted to the floor that the toilet mounts on. That wax makes the seal that keeps water and sewer gas where they belong. It has no moving parts. It does not dry out down there and it does not wear out on any schedule. Left alone, the ring you have

[14:13] right now will outlive the roof, the furnace, and most of the appliances in the house. So, when somebody looks at a solid, steady toilet and says the wax ring is due for replacement, keep your wallet in your pocket because wax does not have a due date. It fails for exactly one reason, movement. A toilet that rocks even a little grinds against that seal every time somebody sits down and sooner or later, the wax lets go. Then come the real warning signs, water seeping out around the base after a

[14:43] flush, a stain spreading on the ceiling under an upstairs bathroom, or a sewer smell that comes and goes. The old way is to stop the rocking long before any of that. Snug the two bolts base gently since porcelain cracks if you hog them down and slip plastic shims, the thin wedges from the hardware aisle, under the low spots until the toilet sits dead still. A pack of shims costs a couple of dollars while a plumber's visit to pull and reset a toilet runs a couple hundred. If the seal has already failed,

[15:14] the ring itself is a cheap part and a Saturday morning of work. And if you pull the toilet and find the flange underneath cracked or rotted below floor level, that is honest work for a licensed plumber, and I will tell you so every time. A toilet that sits still keeps its seal, and a seal left alone keeps its promise for a generation. Before you go hunting shims, the drain book I mentioned, 101 Ways to Unclog Any Drain, is a digital download that covers clogs the way we covered toilets today.

[3:33] a bit of physics. Now, watch the play. With that valve standing guard on the outlet, the plumber turns his tank up to 140°, then sets the valve to send the house a steady 120. [4:33] the flinch. That's the reason the standard advice says 120 at the tap. And it's good advice. So hear me on this one. Do not walk downstairs tonight and spin that dial to 140 on its own. [0:00] Ever walk into your bathroom and notice a little puddle of water sitting around the base of your toilet? Maybe you smell something you shouldn't be smelling, and you think, "Well, I just installed that thing last month. How is it leaking already?" I'll tell you. Nine times out of 10, it's not a bad toilet. It's a bad install. And the frustrating part is, most of these mistakes take about 2 seconds to make and cost you hundreds of dollars in

[0:30] water damage. In this video, I'm going to walk you through the complete toilet installation process from start to finish. And along the way, I'll point out 12 mistakes that practically guarantee a leak. Stick around to the end because I've got a bonus tip that'll help you catch a hidden leak before it ever ruins your subfloor. Let's get to work. Here's the thing about installing a toilet. It's not a complicated job, but it's an unforgiving one.

[1:01] There's no wiggle room on the details, and every shortcut you take is a leak waiting to happen. The good news is, once you understand how the seal actually works, and you take your time with each step, this is absolutely something you can handle on your own. So, let's talk about how your toilet seals to the drain. Underneath the base of every toilet, there's a round opening called the horn. That horn sits down into a fitting

[1:32] called the closet flange, which is bolted to your floor and connected to the drain pipe below. Between the horn and the flange, you've got a wax ring that creates a watertight seal. Every single time you flush, water and waste push past that seal and down into the drain. If that seal isn't perfect, you've got sewage gas, water damage, or both creeping into your bathroom. That's why every step in this process

[2:03] matters. The flange, the wax ring, the way you set the bowl, how you tighten the bolts, all of it works together to create one solid, leak-free seal. All right, the first thing you need to do before a new toilet goes anywhere near that flange is inspect what you're working with. Get down there with a flashlight and really look at your closet flange. Is it cracked? Is it corroded? Are the

[2:35] bolt slots broken out? If your flange is damaged, no wax ring in the world is going to save you. That's mistake number one. People set a brand new toilet right on top of a broken flange and wonder why it leaks 3 weeks later. If your flange is cracked or missing pieces, you need to replace it or install a repair ring before you go any further. They make stainless steel repair flanges that bolt right over the old one and

[3:06] they work great. Now, here's mistake number two and this one's just as common. Your flange needs to be sitting at the right height. The top of that flange should be flush with your finished floor or just barely above it, about an eighth of an inch. If someone tiled or put new flooring down and didn't raise the flange, now it's sitting a quarter inch or even a half inch below the floor surface.

[3:36] That means your wax ring has to bridge a gap it was never designed for and you'll get a weak seal every time. If your flange is too low, pick up a flange extender kit. They're cheap, they stack right on top, and they'll bring you back up to the correct height. Take your time on this one. Getting the flange right is the foundation of everything else. Once your flange is solid and sitting at the right height, let's talk about the

[4:07] wax ring. This is where mistakes three and four live. Mistake number three is grabbing the wrong wax ring. They're not all the same. For a standard installation where your flange is flush with the floor, a regular wax ring works fine. But if your flange is sitting even slightly below the finished floor, you want a wax ring with a built-in polyethylene funnel. Sometimes called an extra thick or

[4:38] extended wax ring. That funnel directs everything straight down into the drain and gives you a more reliable seal when the gap is a little wider than normal. Read the packaging, match it to your situation, and don't just grab the cheapest one on the shelf. Mistake number four is something I've seen more times than I can count. Somebody figures if one wax ring is good, two must be better. They stack two standard wax rings on top

[5:08] of each other thinking it'll make a stronger seal. Here's what actually happens. When you press the toilet down, those two rings shift against each other, and instead of one solid seal, you've got two layers that can't bond properly. If your flange is so low that a single extra thick ring won't reach, you need a flange extender, not a wax ring sandwich. Don't let anybody tell you different.

[5:38] By the way, if you're finding this advice helpful, do me a small favor and hit that subscribe button down below. I put out new videos like this every week, walking you through repairs that'll save you a small fortune in plumber bills. Subscribing is free and it's the best way to make sure you don't miss the next one. All right, let's continue. Now, before you commit to setting the toilet, here's where mistake number five trips people up.

[6:08] They skip the dry fit. Before you put any wax ring down, take your toilet bowl and set it over the flange without any wax. Just lower it into position and see how it sits. Does it rock? Is it stable? Does the horn line up with the flange opening? If the bowl rocks on the floor, you've got an uneven surface and that's a problem you need to solve now, not after the wax is compressed. A rocking toilet will eventually break

[6:38] its wax seal. You can shim it with plastic toilet shims, the kind you can snap off and trim flush. Get it stable during the dry fit, then pull it back off and proceed with confidence. Now comes the moment of truth, setting the toilet onto the wax ring. Drop your new closet bolts into the flange slots and make sure they're standing straight up. Set your wax ring on top of the flange, centered over the drain opening.

[7:08] Now, here's mistake number six and it's a big one. When you lower the toilet, you need to go straight down. I mean, perfectly straight. Line up the bolt holes on the base of the toilet with those closet bolts and lower it evenly. Do not twist the bowl. Do not slide it around trying to find the bolts. Every time you twist or shift that bowl on the wax, you're smearing the seal and creating a path for water to escape.

[7:40] That's mistake number seven for good measure. One smooth, straight drop. Then press down firmly with your body weight. Rocking gently front to back if needed, but never side to side. You want that wax to compress evenly all the way around. With the bowl sitting on a good wax seal, it's time for the closet bolts. Slip your washers on, thread the nuts down, and here's where mistakes eight and nine come in.

[8:11] Mistake number eight is the one that breaks hearts and porcelain. People crank down on those nuts like they're tightening lug nuts on a truck. Porcelain is strong under compression, but it's brittle under point stress. One extra quarter turn and you'll hear a crack that'll make your stomach drop because now you're buying a whole new toilet. What I do is tighten each nut a little at a time, alternating side to side. A quarter turn on the left, a

[8:42] quarter turn on the right. Back and forth. You want the toilet snug against the floor with no rocking. That's it. Mistake number nine is only tightening one side, which pulls the bowl off center and creates an uneven seal. Alternate sides. Be patient. When it feels firm and the bowl doesn't move, stop. Next up is the water supply line. This is where mistakes 10 and 11 happen, and

[9:12] they're both easy to avoid. Mistake number 10 is over-tightening the supply line connection at the fill valve. That nut at the bottom of the tank is plastic. You hand-tighten it, then give it a quarter turn with your channel locks. That's all. Any more than that and you'll strip the threads or crack the fitting. Mistake number 11 is reusing your old supply line. I don't care if it looks fine. Those braided stainless steel supply lines cost about $6 and the

[9:44] rubber inside them degrades over time. A new toilet deserves a new supply line. It's cheap insurance against a flood. Get a 3/8 inch compression by 7/8 inch ballcock connector and make sure it's long enough to reach without kinking. I usually go with a 12-in line for most standard installs. Now, turn the water on slowly and let the tank fill up. Flush it a couple times and get down on the floor with a flashlight. Check

[10:14] around the base. Check the supply connection at the tank. Check where the supply meets the shutoff valve. No drips anywhere? Good. Now, here's mistake number 12 and it's the one people argue about. You need to caulk around the base of the toilet. Some folks think caulking traps water under there. Here's the truth. Most plumbing codes actually require it and caulking prevents water from mopping, spills, or a shower from

[10:45] seeping underneath the toilet and rotting your subfloor. What I do is leave a small gap at the back, maybe an inch or two, uncaulked. That way, if you ever did have a wax ring failure, water would seep out the back and you'd catch it early instead of it hiding under the caulk for months. Use a kitchen and bath silicone caulk. Lay a clean bead, smooth it with a wet finger, and you're done. Let's talk about some common problems

[11:16] you might run into after the install and how to handle them. First one is a toilet that rocks even after you've shimmed and tightened the bolts. If it's still moving, your flange might be sitting too high holding the base off the floor. You might need to trim the flange down or add flooring material to bring the floor up to match. Second issue is a slow leak at the base that only shows up when you flush. That's almost always a wax ring that

[11:47] didn't seat properly. You'll need to pull the toilet and reset it with a new wax ring. And yes, you need a new ring every time. You can never reuse a wax ring. Third one is water trickling from the supply connection at the tank. Try snugging the nut just a hair more with your channel locks. If it still drips, pull the line off, check that the rubber washer inside the coupling is seated flat, and reconnect.

[12:18] Fourth problem is condensation on the tank that drips down and looks like a leak. That's not a plumbing issue. That's humidity. A toilet tank anti-sweat valve mixes a little warm water into the fill and solves it. Or, you can insulate the inside of the tank with a foam liner kit. Fifth is a toilet that runs constantly after the install. That's a flapper or fill valve issue, not related to your installation, but it's worth adjusting the float height

[12:49] and checking the flapper seat before you button everything up. Take 5 minutes and make sure the tank internals are working right. All right, here's that bonus tip I promised you. [13:19] around the base with a paper towel. If you see any color on that paper towel, you've got a seal issue that needs attention before it becomes a real problem.
